STATE OF FLORIDA

AUDITOR GENERAL
TALLAHASSEE
CHARLES L. LESTER, CPA
AUDITOR GENERAL
May 29, 1998

The President of the Senate, the Speaker of the
House of Representatives, and the
Legislative Auditing Committee

Pursuant to the provisions of Section 11.45, Florida Statutes, I have directed that an
audit be made of the

FEDERAL AWARDS PROGRAMS
OF THE
STATE OF FLORIDA

For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997,

and present this report thereon.
INTRODUCTION

During the 1996-1997 fiscal year, expenditures of Federal funds totaled approximately
$10 billion for the State of Florida. Each of the Federal programs under which Florida
received Federal awards operates pursuant to distinct Federal laws and regulations and a
demonstration of compliance with these laws is a prerequisite for continued Federal financial
participation. Audits have long been the means by which the Federal Government obtained a
measure of grantee compliance and, in order to provide for a uniform audit approach, the
Single Audit Act of 1984 (Public Law 98-502) was implemented. On July 5, 1996, the Single
Audit Act of 1984 was replaced by Public Law 104-156, referred to as the Single Audit Act
Amendments of 1996. The Single Audit Act Amendments of 1996 are effective for audits of
fiscal years that begin after June 30, 1996. Accordingly, this audit of the Federal awards
programs for the State’s 1996-97 fiscal year was conducted pursuant to the amended Single

Audit Act.

---

## Page 16

The stated purposes of the Single Audit Act, as amended, are: (1) to promote sound
financial management, including effective internal controls, with respect to Federal awards
administered by non-Federal entities; (2) to establish uniform requirements for audits of
Federal awards administered by non-Federal entities; (3) to promote the efficient and effective
use of audit resources; (4) to reduce burdens on State and local governments, Indian tribes, and
nonprofit organizations; and (5) to ensure that Federal departments and agencies, to the
maximum extent practicable, rely upon and use audit work done pursuant to the amended

Single Audit Act.

Reporting Package

Pursuant to United States Office of Management and Budget (OMB) Circular A-133, an
audit reporting package must be assembled and the appropriate number of copies of that
package submitted to the designated Federal clearinghouse as well as applicable Federal
agencies and pass-through entities. The reporting package is to be comprised of the: (1)
audited entity’s financial statements and schedule of expenditures of Federal awards; (2)
audited entity’s summary schedule of prior audit findings; (3) auditor’s report; and (4) audited
entity’s corrective action plan. For this audit, the State of Florida’s audited Schedule of
Expenditures of Federal Awards and the State of Florida’s Corrective Action Plan are
incorporated within this document (i.e., the auditor’s report). The audited Schedule of
Expenditures of Federal Awards is contained in Section 2 of this document. The Corrective
Action Plans of the various entities, for which audit findings are reported in the Schedule of
Findings and Questioned Costs, are incorporated within the statements from audited officials in
Sections 4, 5, and 6 of this report. The State of Florida’s audited general purpose financial
statements and Summary Schedule of Prior Audit Findings are not incorporated within this

document but are submitted as separate parts of the reporting package.

Major Federal Awards Programs

A Federal awards program is defined by OMB Circular A-133 as all Federal awards
assigned a single number in the Federal Catalog of Federal Domestic Assistance (CFDA). The
CFDA is a government-wide compendium of individual Federal programs for which each
program contained therein is assigned a unique five-digit program identification number

(CFDA number). When no CFDA number has been assigned, OMB Circular A-133 provides

---

## Page 17

that all Federal awards granted by the same agency for the same purpose should be combined
and considered one program. Notwithstanding those definitions, OMB Circular A-133 provides
that a Federal program may also be defined as a cluster of programs. A cluster of programs is
defined as a grouping of closely related programs that have different CFDA numbers but share
common compliance requirements. A cluster of programs shall be considered as one program

for purposes of determining major programs as explained in the following paragraphs.

The Single Audit Act Amendments of 1996 provide for the audit of an entity’s major
Federal awards programs. To determine the Federal programs that should be classified as
major programs the auditor is required to use a risk-based approach. Under the risk-based
approach, all Federal programs are first separated into two classes based on expenditure levels:
Type A programs and Type B programs. Type A programs represent those programs with
relatively higher expenditure levels and Type B programs represent those programs with
relatively lower expenditure levels. For the State of Florida, the 1996-97 fiscal year threshold
(i.e., expenditure level) that distinguished Type A programs from Type B programs was
$28,126,595. In accordance with OMB Circular A-133, that threshold was determined by
multiplying a predetermined factor times total Federal awards expended by the State during

that fiscal year.

After identifying each program as either a Type A or Type B program, the auditor must
classify each Type A program and “larger” Type B programs as either high-risk or low-risk
based upon a consideration of: (1) current and prior audit experience, (2) oversight exercised
by Federal agencies and pass-through entities, and (3) the inherent risk of the Federal program.
For the 1996-97 fiscal year, the State of Florida’s “larger” Type B programs are defined as
those Type B programs with expenditures exceeding a threshold of $2,812,660, as determined
by applying a predetermined factor to total Federal awards expended in accordance with
criteria established in OMB Circular A-133. At a minimum, the auditor must audit as major
programs all high-risk Type A Programs and either: (1) at least one-half of the high-risk
(larger) Type B programs, except that the auditor is not required to audit more high-risk Type
B programs than the number of low-risk Type A programs identified or (2) one high-risk Type
B program for each low-risk Type A program identified. Furthermore, the auditor must

ensure that those programs audited as major programs represent Federal programs with

---

## Page 18

Federal awards expended that, in the aggregate, encompass at least 50 percent of total Federal

awards expended by the audited entity.

For the State of Florida’s 1996-97 fiscal year, there were 29 Type A programs and 64
larger Type B programs that would be subject to the risk assessment process described above.
In addition, there were approximately 400 “smaller” Type B programs that would not be

subject to that risk assessment process.

As provided by OMB Circular A-133, we have elected to audit as major programs all
Type A programs for the State of Florida’s first-year audit (i.e., 1996-97 fiscal year) under the
Single Audit Act Amendments of 1996. Under this first-year option, no Type B programs
were required to be audited as major programs. However, we also elected to audit as a major
program one Type B program (i.e., the Refugee and Entrant Assistance — State Administered
Programs) because of continuing material deficiencies reported in recent audit reports relating
to that program. In our subsequent audits of the State of Florida, the major programs will be

determined using the risk assessment process prescribed in OMB Circular A-133.

The 29 State of Florida Type A programs and the one Type B program audited as
major programs for the 1996-97 fiscal year were administered by the following 13 State

agencies/institutions:

Auditor’s Results.

Prior Audit Findings

In addition to the audit of an entity’s major programs, OMB Circular A-133 requires
the auditor to follow up on audit findings of prior audits of the entity’s Federal awards
programs, regardless of whether those prior audit findings pertain to programs determined by

the auditor to be major programs in the current audit. The auditor is also required to assess

---

## Page 20

the reasonableness of the status of those prior audit findings as reported by the audited entity
on the summary schedule of prior audit findings. The prior audit findings for which we were
required to follow up on as part of this audit are listed in the State of Florida’s Summary
Schedule of Prior Audit Findings. As noted above, the State’s Summary Schedule of Prior
Audit Findings is not incorporated in this document but is submitted as a separate part of the

reporting package.

Scope and Objectives

The scope of our Single Audit encompassed the general purpose financial statements of
the State of Florida reporting entity as disclosed in note 1 to the general purpose financial
statements (included in audit report No. 13138, dated January 29, 1998). Pursuant to

Governmental Accounting Standards Board (GASB) Statement No. 14, The Financial

Reporting Entity, the State of Florida reporting entity includes both the primary government

and those organizations determined to be component units. Component units are defined as
organizations for which the primary government is financially accountable or for which the
nature and significance of their relationship with the primary government are such that
exclusion would cause the primary government’s financial statements to be misleading or
incomplete. The vast majority of the component units of the State of Florida are discretely
presented in the State of Florida’s general purpose financial statements. Under a discrete
presentation, the financial information for the component units is presented separately from the
financial information of the primary government. Component units not discretely presented
are reported as if they are part of the primary government. Note 1 to the general purpose

financial statements addresses the various component units.

In regard to the management of the State of Florida’s Federal awards programs, the
scope of our Single Audit encompassed the State of Florida primary government and the State
Community College System, a discretely presented component unit for financial statement
presentation purposes. We did not audit the management of the Federal awards programs
administered by the Legislature or the Florida Housing Finance Agency (each organizationally
part of the primary government), or the remaining discretely presented component units (i.e.,
other than the State Community College System). As applicable, the management of Federal

awards programs administered by these agencies is the subject of audits completed by other

---

## Page 21

auditors. Total Federal awards expended by the Legislature and the Florida Housing Finance
Agency constituted less than one percent of total Federal awards expended by the State of
Florida primary government for the fiscal year ended June 30, 1997. Furthermore, total
Federal awards expended by the Legislature and the Florida Housing Finance Agency under
major programs constituted less than one percent of total Federal awards expended by the State
of Florida primary government under the respective major program for the fiscal year ended
June 30, 1997. In addition, an audit of the accounts and records of those organizations
receiving Federal awards from the State of Florida primary government as subrecipients, other

than the State’s community colleges, was not included within the scope of our audit.

In accordance with the Single Audit Act Amendments of 1996 and OMB Circular
A-133, the objectives of our Single Audit of the State of Florida, for those agencies/institutions

included within the scope of our audit, were as follows:

e Determine whether the general purpose financial statements of the State of Florida,
included in audit report No. 13138, dated January 29, 1998, present fairly, in all
material respects, its financial position as of June 30, 1997, and the results of its
operations, and the cash flows of its proprietary fund types, nonexpendable trust
fund, and discretely presented component units for the year then ended, in

accordance with generally accepted accounting principles;

e Determine whether the schedule of expenditures of Federal awards included in this
report is fairly stated in all material respects in relation to the general purpose

financial statements taken as a whole;

e Obtain an understanding of internal control over Federal awards programs sufficient
to plan the audit to support a low assessed level of control risk for major Federal
awards programs; and to determine the nature, timing, and extent of tests to be
performed for the purpose of determining whether the State of Florida is managing
major Federal awards programs in compliance with laws, regulations, and the
provisions of contracts or grant agreements that may have a direct and material

effect on those programs;

---

## Page 22

e Determine whether management of the State of Florida has complied with laws,
regulations, and the provisions of contracts or grant agreements that may have a

direct and material effect on the general purpose financial statements:

e Determine whether management of the State of Florida has complied with laws,
regulations, and the provisions of contracts or grant agreements that may have a

direct and material effect on each major Federal awards program; and

e Determine whether management of the State of Florida has resolved prior year audit
findings relating to Federal awards programs and fairly represented the status of

those prior audit findings on the State’s summary schedule of prior audit findings.

The State of Florida has elected to conduct its Single Audit in two phases: (1) an
examination of the general purpose financial statements and a determination of the extent of
management compliance with laws, regulations, and provisions of contracts and grant
agreements noncompliance with which could have a direct and material effect on the general
purpose financial statements and (2) an audit of the management of Federal awards programs.
The findings relating to our audit of the State’s general purpose financial statements are
presented in audit report No. 13138, dated January 29, 1998. Findings relating to our audit of

the State’s Federal awards programs are presented in the following sections of this report.

Methodology

Our audit was conducted in accordance with generally accepted auditing standards;
Government Auditing Standards issued by the Comptroller General of the United States; the
Single Audit Act Amendments of 1996; and OMB Circular A-133.

As indicated above, our audit was conducted in two phases. The first phase resulted in
our reports dated January 29, 1998 (Auditor General audit report No. 13138), addressing the
fairness of the State’s general purpose financial statement representations, the results of our
consideration of the internal control in order to determine auditing procedures for the purpose
of expressing an opinion on the general purpose financial statements, and the extent of
management compliance with laws, regulations, and provisions of contracts and grant
agreements noncompliance with which could have a direct and material effect on the general

purpose financial statements. The remaining report required by generally accepted auditing
8

---

## Page 23

standards, Government Auditing Standards, the Single Audit Act Amendments of 1996, and

OMB Circular A-133 is included in Section 1 of this report.

As required by the Single Audit Act Amendments of 1996 and OMB Circular A-133,
our audit was designed to measure compliance with the principal compliance requirements of
major Federal awards programs. These compliance requirements and recommended audit
procedures were ascertained by referring to the OMB CIRCULAR A-133 COMPLIANCE
SUPPLEMENT - PROVISIONAL June 1997 and to applicable Federal laws, regulations, and
provisions of contracts and grant agreements. In some situations agencies have established
additional requirements for fund allocations, determinations of eligibility, and similar matters.
Although not within the scope of this audit, such additional requirements are reviewed
periodically in connection with other audits performed by this Office. To the extent
appropriate, we have relied upon previous audits issued by this Office which addressed, among
other subjects, the management of particular Federal awards programs during the audit period.
These reports are as follows:

As disclosed previously, the scope of our audit did not include an examination of the
accounts and records of those organizations receiving Federal awards from the State of Florida
primary government as subrecipients, other than the State’s community colleges. Our audit
did, however, include a review of the systems established by various State agencies to obtain,
review, and follow up on required audits of subrecipients. Based on Federal regulations
applicable to the audit period, these subrecipients would have 13 months within which to
provide audit reports to the primary recipient agencies. Unless otherwise noted, our review
was, therefore, directed at the adequacy of the systems implemented by the various agencies in
regard to audits of moneys provided to subrecipients during the 1995-96 fiscal year and for

which reports would have been due during or subsequent to the 1996-97 fiscal year.

Instances in which investigations/reviews were continuing at the completion of our audit
field work and which involved the possibility of irregularities, abuse, or illegal acts are

disclosed in the Schedule of Findings and Questioned Costs (Schedule 1).

REPORT ON COMPLIANCE WITH REQUIREMENTS APPLICABLE TO EACH MAJOR
PROGRAM, INTERNAL CONTROL OVER COMPLIANCE, AND THE SCHEDULE OF
EXPENDITURES OF FEDERAL AWARDS

Compliance. We have audited the compliance of the State of Florida with the types of
compliance requirements described in the UNITED STATES OFFICE OF MANAGEMENT
AND BUDGET (OMB) CIRCULAR A-133 COMPLIANCE SUPPLEMENT that are
applicable to each of its major Federal programs for the year ended June 30, 1997. The State
of Florida’s major Federal programs are identified in the summary of auditor’s results section
of the accompanying schedule of findings and questioned costs. Compliance with the
requirements of laws, regulations, contracts and grants applicable to each of its major Federal
programs is the responsibility of the management of the State of Florida. Our responsibility is

to express an opinion on the State of Florida’s compliance based on our audit.

The State of Florida’s general purpose financial statements include the operations of
component units which received Federal awards during the fiscal year ended June 30, 1997.
Our audit of Federal awards, as described below, did not extend to those component units
other than the State Community College System. The management of those component units
other than the State Community College System is the subject of audits completed by other

auditors.

Except as discussed in audit report No. 13138, we conducted our audit of compliance in
accordance with generally accepted auditing standards; the standards applicable to financial

audits contained in Government Auditing Standards issued by the Comptroller General of the

1-1

---

## Page 30

United States; and OMB Circular A-133, AUDITS OF STATES, LOCAL GOVERNMENTS,
AND NON-PROFIT ORGANIZATIONS. Those standards and OMB Circular A-133 require
that we plan and perform the audit to obtain reasonable assurance about whether
noncompliance with the types of compliance requirements referred to above that could have a
direct and material effect on a major Federal program occurred. An audit includes examining,
on a test basis, evidence about the State of Florida’s compliance with those requirements and
performing such other procedures as we considered necessary in the circumstances. We
believe that our audit provides a reasonable basis for our opinion. The legal determination on
the State’s compliance with these requirements is, however, ultimately the responsibility of the

grantor agency.

Material noncompliance represents failure by management to follow program
requirements to a degree that such noncompliance resulting from those failures is material to
the respective programs. As described in the paragraphs within the Schedule of Findings and
Questioned Costs that are identified under the category of “material noncompliance” on the
accompanying SCHEDULE OF MATERIAL NONCOMPLIANCE, OTHER INSTANCES
OF NONCOMPLIANCE, REPORTABLE CONDITIONS, AND MATERIAL

are applicable to some of its major Federal programs, as shown in the following tabulation:

Compliance with such requirements is necessary, in our opinion, for the State of Florida to

We considered the effect of these material instances of noncompliance on whether the

State of Florida’s general purpose financial statements for the fiscal year ended June 30, 1997,

are presented fairly in all material respects, in conformity with generally accepted accounting

principles, and this report does not affect our report dated January 29, 1998, on those general

purpose financial statements.

In our opinion, except for the noncompliance described above, and except for the

possible effects of the instances of noncompliance, if any, which may be disclosed by any

1-3

---

## Page 32

ongoing investigations/reviews as discussed in the accompanying Schedule of Findings and
Questioned Costs, the State of Florida complied, in all material respects, with the requirements
referred to above that are applicable to each of its major Federal programs for the year ended
June 30, 1997. The results of our auditing procedures also disclosed other instances of
noncompliance with those requirements that are required to be reported in accordance with
OMB Circular A-133. Those other instances of noncompliance are described in paragraphs
within the Schedule of Findings and Questioned Costs as identified under the categories of
“other instances of noncompliance” and “reportable conditions” on the accompanying
SCHEDULE OF MATERIAL NONCOMPLIANCE, OTHER INSTANCES OF
NONCOMPLIANCE, REPORTABLE CONDITIONS, AND MATERIAL WEAKNESSES.

Internal Control Over Compliance. The management of the State of Florida is

responsible for establishing and maintaining effective internal control over compliance with
requirements of laws, regulations, contracts and grants applicable to Federal programs. In
planning and performing our audit, we considered the State of Florida’s internal control over
compliance with requirements that could have a direct and material effect on a major Federal
program in order to determine our auditing procedures for the purpose of expressing our
opinion on compliance and to test and report on internal control over compliance in accordance

with OMB Circular A-133.

We noted certain matters involving the internal control over compliance and its
operation that we consider to be reportable conditions. Reportable conditions involve matters
coming to our attention relating to significant deficiencies in the design or operation of the
internal control over compliance that, in our judgment, could adversely affect the State of
Florida’s ability to administer a major Federal program in accordance with applicable
requirements of laws, regulations, contracts, and grants. Reportable conditions are described
in the paragraphs within the Schedule of Findings and Questioned Costs that are identified
under the category of “reportable conditions” on the accompanying SCHEDULE OF
MATERIAL NONCOMPLIANCE, OTHER INSTANCES OF NONCOMPLIANCE,
REPORTABLE CONDITIONS, AND MATERIAL WEAKNESSES. Also, ongoing
investigations as described in the accompanying Schedule of Findings and Questioned Costs

may disclose additional reportable conditions.

1-4

---

## Page 33

A material weakness is a condition in which the design or operation of one or more of
the internal control components does not reduce to a relatively low level the risk that
noncompliance with applicable requirements of laws, regulations, contracts, and grants that
would be material in relation to a major Federal program being audited may occur and not be
detected within a timely period by employees in the normal course of performing their assigned
functions. Our consideration of the internal control over compliance would not necessarily
disclose all matters in the internal control that might be reportable conditions and, accordingly,
would not necessarily disclose all reportable conditions that are considered to be material
weaknesses. However, we considered some of the reportable conditions referenced above to
be material weaknesses as indicated under the category of “material weaknesses” on the
accompanying SCHEDULE OF MATERIAL NONCOMPLIANCE, OTHER INSTANCES
OF NONCOMPLIANCE, REPORTABLE CONDITIONS, AND MATERIAL
WEAKNESSES.

We also noted other matters involving the internal control and its operation which do
not constitute reportable conditions as described above. These, and other matters, are
routinely reported in separate operational audit reports on the various organizational units
composing the State of Florida’s reporting entity. Matters of lesser significance are discussed

with management of the organizational units during the course of our various audits.

Schedule of Expenditures of Federal Awards. We have audited the general purpose

financial statements of the State of Florida as of and for the fiscal year ended June 30, 1997,
and have issued our report thereon dated January 29, 1998. As disclosed in audit report No.
13138, our report on the general purpose financial statements included disclosures regarding
our reference to reports of other auditors, the inadequacy of records to document the
ownership and valuation of a substantial portion of State land reported in the General Fixed
Assets Account Group, and the implementation of GASB Statement Nos. 25, 27, and 28. Our
audit was performed for the purpose of forming an opinion on the general purpose financial
statements taken as a whole. The accompanying Supplementary Schedule of Expenditures of
Federal Awards is presented for purposes of additional analysis as required by OMB Circular
A-133 and is not a required part of the general purpose financial statements. Such information

has been subjected to the auditing procedures applied in the audit of the general purpose

1-5

---

## Page 34

financial statements and, in our opinion, except for the effects that the lack of adequate
accounting records as described in paragraph SA 97-151 of the Schedule of Findings and
Questioned Costs had on the amounts reported as provided to subrecipients outside the State
reporting entity, such information is fairly stated, in all material respects, in relation to the

general purpose financial statements taken as a whole.

This report is intended for the information of the Legislative Auditing Committee,
members of the Florida Senate and the Florida House of Representatives, Federal and other
granting agencies, and applicable management. Copies of this report are available pursuant to

Section 11.45(7), Florida Statutes, and its distribution is not limited.

## Page 82

STATE OF FLORIDA
NOTES TO SUPPLEMENTARY SCHEDULE OF
EXPENDITURES OF FEDERAL AWARDS
For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997

BASIS OF PRESENTATION AND SIGNIFICANT
ACCOUNTING POLICIES

The supplementary schedule of expenditures of Federal awards has been prepared in
accordance with the United States Office of Management and Budget (OMB) Circular A-133
and presents Federal awards expended by the State of Florida. The following summary of the
State of Florida’s significant accounting policies and related information is presented to assist
the reader in interpreting the supplementary schedule of expenditures of Federal awards and

should be viewed as an integral part of the accompanying supplementary schedule.

e Federal Awards

The Single Audit Act Amendments of 1996 (Public Law 104-156) and the OMB Circular
A-133 define Federal awards as Federal financial assistance and Federal
cost-reimbursement contracts that non-Federal entities receive directly from Federal
awarding agencies or indirectly from pass-through entities. Federal financial assistance
is defined as assistance that non-Federal entities receive or administer in the form of
grants, loans, loan guarantees, property, cooperative agreements, interest subsidies,

insurance, food commodities, direct appropriations, or other assistance.

e Catalog of Federal Domestic Assistance Number (CFDA No.)

The Catalog of Federal Domestic Assistance is a governmentwide compendium of
individual Federal programs. A five-digit program identification number (CFDA No.) is
assigned to each program included in the catalog. Those programs which have not been
assigned a CFDA number by the Federal Government and those programs for which
CFDA numbers could not be identified are included in the category “Other Federal
Awards” on the accompanying supplementary schedule of expenditures of Federal

awards. In those instances in which CFDA numbers were not assigned or identified,

---

## Page 83

STATE OF FLORIDA
NOTES TO SUPPLEMENTARY SCHEDULE OF
EXPENDITURES OF FEDERAL AWARDS (CONTINUED)
For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997

other Federal identification numbers are included on the accompanying schedule to the

extent such numbers were assigned by the Federal grantor agency and identified.

Expenditures/Disbursements

Expenditures reported on the accompanying schedule for State agencies consist of
amounts expended from Federal programs by those agencies during the fiscal year ended
June 30, 1997, determined in accordance with the modified accrual basis of accounting.
Therefore, these amounts are consistent with the related presentations in the State of

Florida's general purpose financial statements.

Expenditures reported on the accompanying schedule for the State University System
(SUS) and the State Community College System (SCCS) consist of amounts expended
from Federal programs by the applicable institutions during the fiscal year ended
June 30, 1997, determined in accordance with the cash basis of accounting and the cost
accounting principles contained in OMB Circular No. A-21, COST PRINCIPLES FOR
EDUCATIONAL INSTITUTIONS. Therefore, these amounts may differ from the basis
of presentation (i.e., accrual basis) of the related amounts on the State of Florida's

general purpose financial statements.

Memorandum Total Only Column

The column on the accompanying supplementary schedule and in these notes captioned
"Memorandum Total Only" combines amounts that are presented using different bases of
accounting, and for one program (i.e., the Federal Family Education Loans [CFDA No.
84.032] Program) includes interagency transactions that have not been eliminated.
Accordingly, that column is presented only to facilitate financial analysis and is not
intended to present total Federal awards expenditures of the State of Florida in

accordance with generally accepted accounting principles.

---

## Page 84

STATE OF FLORIDA
NOTES TO SUPPLEMENTARY SCHEDULE OF
EXPENDITURES OF FEDERAL AWARDS (CONTINUED)
For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997

e Transfers to Subrecipients Column

The column on the accompanying supplementary schedule captioned "Transfers to
Subrecipients" represents the amounts transferred by the State agencies, State
universities, and State community colleges to subrecipients that are not included in the
State of Florida reporting entity as determined for this audit (i.e., the State of Florida
primary government and the State Community College System). Transfers, pursuant to
subrecipient relationships, between the State agencies, State universities, and State
community colleges are not included in this column. Furthermore, the amounts in this
column are included in the reported Federal award expenditures under the respective

State Agency, State University System, and State Community College System columns.

e Interagency Transfers

Except for the Federal Family Education Loan (FFEL) Program (CFDA No. 84.032),
appropriate adjustments have been made to the reported expenditures on the
accompanying supplementary schedule for the transfers of Federal awards between the

various State agencies, State universities, and State community colleges.

In regard to the FFEL Program, the net increase in the value of loan guarantees during
the 1996-97 fiscal year, totaling $499,380,502, reported as State Agency Expenditures
under CFDA No. 84.032 includes the increases in the value of loan guarantees reported
for State universities ($287,384,177) and State community colleges ($88,337,898) under
CFDA No. 84.032 within the Student Financial Assistance Programs Cluster.

e Administering Agency

The agencies/institutions reported as the administering agencies on the accompanying
supplementary schedule represent the entities that expended and/or administered the

material amounts of the respective Federal awards programs. Entities that expended

2 - 46

## Page 85

STATE OF FLORIDA
NOTES TO SUPPLEMENTARY SCHEDULE OF
EXPENDITURES OF FEDERAL AWARDS (CONTINUED)
For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997

and/or administered program amounts that were not material to the respective program's
total expenditures are not shown as administering agencies on the accompanying

supplementary schedule.

e Major Programs

OMB Circular A-133 established the criteria and process to be used in determining major
Federal awards programs. A description of those criteria and the process is included in
the introduction to this report. Those Federal awards programs determined to be a major
program or part of a major program are identified on the accompanying supplementary

schedule by an asterisk next to the applicable CFDA number.
2. NONCASH ASSISTANCE

The State of Florida participates in several Federal awards programs in which noncash

benefits are provided through the State to eligible program participants. These include:

e Food Distribution (CFDA Nos. 10.550 and 10.569)

The State of Florida received, for the benefit of the State and its citizens, donated food
from the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA). The value of food
distributed by the Florida Department of Agriculture and Consumer Services (FDACS)
as an agent for the USDA, totaling $38,119,559, is included in the reported program
expenditures on the accompanying supplementary schedule. The distributions during the
fiscal year ended June 30, 1997, were recorded in CFDA No. 10.550, Food Distribution
Program - $31,508,407 and CFDA No. 10.569, Emergency Food Assistance Program -
$6,611,152.

---

## Page 86

STATE OF FLORIDA
NOTES TO SUPPLEMENTARY SCHEDULE OF
EXPENDITURES OF FEDERAL AWARDS (CONTINUED)
For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997

e Food Stamps (CFDA No. 10.551)

During the 1996-97 fiscal year, the Florida Department of Children and Family Services
(FDCFS) administered the Food Stamps Program (CFDA No. 10.551) for the State and
issued food stamps valued at $1,139,360,888. The value of issued food’ stamps is
included on the accompanying supplementary schedule in the amounts reported for the

Food Stamp Programs Cluster.

e Donation of Federal Surplus Personal Property (CFDA No. 39.003)

The Florida Department of Management Services (FDMS) acts as an agent of the United
States General Services Administration (USGSA) in the management and distribution of
Federal surplus tangible personal property. Property valued at $8,361,026 (valued at
23.3 percent of the Federal Government’s reported original acquisition cost as prescribed
by Federal guidelines) was transferred to the State of Florida during the 1996-97 fiscal
year. This amount is included in the expenditures reported on the accompanying
supplementary schedule under the Donation of Federal Surplus Personal Property

Program (CFDA No. 39.003).

e Capitalization Grants for State Revolving
Funds (CFDA No. 66.458)

The Florida Department of Environmental Protection (FDEP) administered the
Capitalization Grants for State Revolving Funds Program (CFDA No. 66.458) during the
1996-97 fiscal year. Under this program, loans are made to local governments to assist
in the costs of financing wastewater treatment facilities. During the period July 1, 1996,
through June 30, 1997, loans totaling $100,725,733, were awarded to local governments
from State and Federal funds for the Capitalization Grants for State Revolving Funds
Program. At June 30, 1997, the cumulative loans awarded totaled $733,143,997, and

the outstanding loan balances totaled $570,860,748. Loan repayments during the period

2-48

---

## Page 87

STATE OF FLORIDA
NOTES TO SUPPLEMENTARY SCHEDULE OF
EXPENDITURES OF FEDERAL AWARDS (CONTINUED)
For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997

July 1, 1996, through June 30, 1997, totaled $37,576,230. The current and cumulative
loan amounts and the amount of outstanding loans are not included in the accompanying
supplementary schedule. However, Federal moneys provided to local governments
during the State 1996-97 fiscal year from current and prior-year awards for the
construction of wastewater treatment facilities, in the amount of $66,730,834, are

included in the accompanying supplementary schedule.

Federal Family Education Loans (CFDA No. 84.032)

The Florida Department of Education (FDOE) administers the Federal Family Education
Loans Program (CFDA No. 84.032) for the State. Under the program, the United States
Department of Education (USDOE) guarantees the repayment of loans made to students
by participating financial institutions. At June 30, 1997, $2,838,375,401 in loans were
outstanding under the program. That amount is not included in the accompanying
supplementary schedule. The net increase in the loan guarantees provided by the
USDOE during the 1996-97 fiscal year, totaling $499,380,502, is included as a separate
item in the amounts reported on the accompanying supplementary schedule. Payments
made during the 1996-97 fiscal year to cover student loans in default are also included in
the State agency amounts reported on the accompanying supplementary schedule. In
addition, the State incurs expenses for the administration of the program. During the
1996-97 fiscal year, the FDOE received from the USDOE an administrative cost
allowance of $5,848,581 for these expenses. Disbursements of those administrative
funds are also included in the amounts reported on the accompanying supplementary

schedule.

Childhood Immunization Grants (CFDA No. 93.268)

During the 1996-97 fiscal year, the Florida Department of Health (FDOH) administered
the Childhood Immunization Grants Program (CFDA No. 93.268) for the State. The

During the fiscal year ended June 30, 1997, the Florida ‘epartment of Health (FDOH)
received cash rebates from infant formula Méiwiufacturers, in ithe amount of $65,385,079.11,
on sales of formula to Patticipants in the Special Supplemental ‘Nutrition Program for Women,
Infants, and Children (WIC) (CFDA No. 10.557). Rebate ‘sontracts with infant formula
manufacturers are authorized by Title 7, Section 246.16(m), Code of Federal Regulations, as
a Cost-containment measure. Rebates represent a reduction of expenditures previously
incurred for WIC food benefit Costs. Based on the average monthly benefit, applying the

rebates received to such costs enabled the FDOH to extend progriim benefits to the equivalent

rebate contract.

unemployment taxes that are deposited into the State's account in the Federal Unemployment
Trust Fund (FUTF). The State's administrative expenditures incurred under this program are
funded by Federal grants. Expenditures reported on the accompanying supplementary
schedule of expenditures of Federal awards for the UI Program include only those made from
Federal funds. State funds disbursed during the 1996-97 fiscal year from the State's account
in the FUTF, which are not included in the amount which appears on the accompanying

supplementary schedule for the UI Program, totaled $1,319,056,469.
SUBSEQUENT EVENTS

The Florida Housing Finance Agency (FHFA), organizationally part of the State of Florida
primary government during the 1996-97 fiscal year, administered Federal funds (programs
other than major programs) provided by the Florida Department of Community Affairs
(FDCA). The Legislature of the State of Florida enacted legislation effective subsequent to
the 1996-97 fiscal year that created within the FDCA a public corporation named "Florida
Housing Finance Corporation" and eliminaed the FHFA. The Florida Housing Finance
Corporation is functionally related to the FDCA but is not part of the State of Florida primary
government. Chapter 97-167, Laws of Florida, provided that as of July 1, 1997, a transitional
period would occur prior to the transfer of al assets from the FHFA to the corporation.
Effective January 1, 1998, the FHFPA assets aid liabilities were transferred to the Florida

Housing Finance Corporation.

## Page 151

PART I
STATE OF FLORIDA
SUMMARY OF AUDITOR’S RESULTS
For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997

Financial Statements

On January 29, 1998, we issued audit report No. 13138, presenting the results of our
audit of the State of Florida’s general purpose financial statements for the fiscal year ended
June 30, 1997. In that audit report, we addressed the fairness of the State’s general purpose
financial statement representations, the results of our consideration of the internal control in
order to determine the auditing procedures necessary for the purpose of expressing an opinion
on the general purpose financial statements, and the extent of management compliance with
laws, regulations, and provisions of contracts and grant agreements noncompliance with which

could have a direct and material effect on the general purpose financial statements.

Two reportable conditions, one of which represented a material weakness, were
disclosed in audit report No. 13138. The material weakness related to the absence of records
supporting the existence and valuation of a substantial portion of land reported in the State’s
General Fixed Assets Account Group. That material weakness resulted in our qualified
opinion on the fair presentation of the State’s general purpose financial statements. The results
of that audit disclosed no instances of noncompliance which were material to the State’s

general purpose financial statements.

Federal Awards Programs

This audit of the State’s major Federal awards programs for the fiscal year ended June
30, 1997, disclosed reportable conditions in the internal control over certain programs. Some
of the reportable conditions represented material weaknesses. These material weaknesses
contributed to instances of material noncompliance and/or questioned costs relative to certain
major Federal awards programs; thereby, resulting in our qualified opinion on compliance in

regard to those programs. These material weaknesses included the following:

e For the Food Stamp Programs Cluster (CFDA Nos. 10.551 and 10.561)
administered by the FDCFS, internal control policies and procedures were not

sufficient to ensure that eligibility and benefit level determinations were proper,

3-1

---

## Page 152

adequate documentation regarding eligibility and benefit levels was maintained, and

salary costs were properly charged.

For the Employment and Training Assistance - Dislocated Workers and Job
Training Partnership Act Programs Cluster (CFDA Nos. 17.246 and 17.250)
administered by the FDLES, internal control policies and procedures were not
sufficient to ensure that funds were used only in compliance with program
regulations, and to ensure accurate reporting of information, including information
that is necessary to demonstrate compliance with applicable matching, level of

effort, and earmarking requirements.

For the Childhood Immunization Grants Program (CFDA No. 93.268) administered
by the FDOH, internal control policies and procedures were not sufficient to

document compliance with maintenance of effort requirements.

For the Temporary Assistance for Needy Families and Family Support Payments to
States — Assistance Payments Programs Cluster (CFDA Nos. 93.558 and 93.560)
administered by the FDCFS, internal control policies and procedures were not
sufficient to ensure that eligibility and benefit level determinations were proper and

to ensure client compliance with program requirements.

For the Child Support Enforcement Program (CFDA No. 93.563) administered by
the FDOR, the State’s internal control policies and procedures were not sufficient to
ensure the timely establishment of case files and initiation/completion of required
case actions, to ensure required and accurate data was reported to the Federal

Government, and to ensure periodic reconciliation of collections were performed.

For the Refugee and Entrant Assistance - State Administered Programs (CFDA No.
93.566) administered by the FDCFS, internal control policies and procedures were
not sufficient to ensure that cash and medical benefits were paid only to or on behalf
of eligible clients, paid only in the proper amounts, and paid only within the periods
allowed by controlling regulations. In addition, FDCFS internal controls were not

sufficient to ensure the preparation and submission of accurate performance reports.

3-2

---

## Page 153

e For the Block Grants for Prevention and Treatment of Substance Abuse Program
(CFDA No. 93.959) administered by the FDCFS, internal control policies and
procedures were not sufficient to ensure compliance with earmarking and period of
availability requirements, to ensure that administrative funds were expended only for
allowable purposes and/or adequately documented, and to ensure that reports were

accurate and/or properly supported by applicable records.

The material weaknesses noted above and the associated instances of material
noncompliance and/or questioned costs are described in more detail in Part III of this Schedule
of Findings and Questioned Costs. In addition to these material weaknesses and instances of
material noncompliance and/or questioned costs, our audit disclosed other findings required to
be reported pursuant to Section .510(a) of OMB Circular A-133. These findings are also

reported in Part III of this Schedule of Findings and Questioned Costs.

In accordance with Section .520(1) of OMB Circular A-133, we have elected to audit as
major programs all Type A programs for this initial year that the State of Florida is audited
under the Single Audit Act Amendments of 1996. For the 1996-97 fiscal year, the threshold
for distinguishing Type A programs from Type B programs was $28,126,595. As a result, all
programs with expenditures greater than that threshold represented major programs for
purposes of this audit. In addition to the Type A programs, we have elected to audit as a
major program one Type B program (i.e., the Refugee and Entrant Assistance - State
Administered Programs) because of continuing material deficiencies reported in recent audit
reports relating to that program. The following brief synopsis identifies these major programs
and the objectives generally attributed to these programs by the applicable Federal grantor

agencies:

Objecti

To improve the diets of school and preschool children; the
elderly; needy persons in charitable institutions; other
individuals in need of food assistance; and to increase the
market for domestically produced foods acquired under surplus
removal or price support operations.

To help low-income households buy the food they need for good
health.

To assist states, through cash grants and food donations, in
providing a nutritious nonprofit breakfast service and in making
the school lunch program available to school students, and to
encourage the domestic consumption of nutritious agricultural
commodities.

To provide, at no cost, supplemental nutritious foods, nutrition
education, and referrals to health care to low-income pregnant,
breastfeeding and postpartum women, infants, and children to
age five determined to be at nutritional risk.

To assist states, through grants-in-aid and other means, to
initiate and maintain nonprofit food service programs for
children and elderly or impaired adults in nonresidential day
care facilities.

To develop viable urban communities by providing decent
housing, a suitable living environment, and expanding economic
opportunities, principally for persons of low and moderate
income.

To place persons in employment by providing a variety of
placement-related services without charge to job seekers and to
employers seeking qualified individuals to fill job openings.

To administer a program of unemployment insurance for eligible
workers through Federal and state cooperation; to administer
payment of trade adjustment assistance; disaster unemployment
assistance; unemployment compensation for Federal employees
and ex-service members.

(1) To assist dislocated workers obtain unsubsidized
employment through training and related employment services
using primarily a decentralized system of state and local
programs; and (2) to establish programs to prepare youth and
adults facing serious barriers to employment for participation in
the labor force by providing job training and other services that
will result in increased employment and earnings, increased
educational and occupational skillls, and decreased welfare
dependency.

(1) To assist states in constructing and rehabilitating the
National Highway System, including the Interstate System; (2)
to assist states in making transportation improvements to all
public roads except those classified as local or rural minor
collectors; (3) to provide aid for the repair of Federal-aid roads
following disasters; (4) to foster safe highway design; (5) to
replace or rehabilitate deficient or obsolete bridges; and (6) to
provide for other special purposes.

To create State Revolving Funds through a program of
capitalization grants to states which will provide a long term
source of state financing for construction of wastewater

management activities.

To provide supplemental assistance to states, local governments,
certain private nonprofit organizations and individuals in
alleviating suffering and hardship resulting from major disasters
or emergencies declared by the President.

To help local education agencies and schools to improve the
teaching and learning of children failing, or most at-risk of

To provide grants to assist states in providing a free appropriate
public education to all children with disabilities.

To encourage lenders such as banks, credit unions, savings and
loan associations, pension funds, insurance companies, and
schools to make loans to vocational, undergraduate, and
graduate students enrolled at eligible postsecondary institutions
to help pay for educational expenses.

## Page 159

PART II
STATE OF FLORIDA
FINDINGS RELATIVE TO THE AUDIT OF THE GENERAL PURPOSE
FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997

Our audit of the State of Florida’s general purpose financial statements for the fiscal
year ended June 30, 1997, disclosed two findings required to be reported in accordance with
Government Auditing Standards issued by the Comptroller General of the United States. As
reported in audit report No. 13138, dated January 29, 1998, these two findings represented
reportable conditions that, in our judgment, could adversely affect the State’s ability to record,
process, summarize, and report financial data consistent with the assertions of management in

the State’s general purpose financial statements. These two reportable conditions are:

e Records of the Board of Trustees of the Internal Improvement Trust Fund were not
adequate to document the ownership and valuation of a substantial portion of land,
which was stated at $2.243 billion and constituted 35 percent of the total value of
general fixed assets reported in the General Fixed Assets Account Group at June 30,
1997. As a result, it was not practicable in the circumstances for us to determine
whether the amount reported for land in the General Fixed Assets Account Group is
fairly presented. This matter represents a material weakness and is discussed in, and
the auditee’s responses are included in, audit report No. 12451, titled Operational
Compliance Audit of the Administration of State-Owned Lands by the Florida
Department of Environmental Protection and Other State Agencies. This matter was
also reported in audits of the State of Florida’s general purpose financial statements
for previous fiscal years, most recently in audit report No. 12907 which covered the

fiscal year ended June 30, 1996.

e The Florida Department of Management Services’ system of internal control, which
encompasses those controls of the Department’s third-party administrators, over the

PART Ill
STATE OF FLORIDA
FINDINGS AND QUESTIONED COSTS FOR FEDERAL AWARDS
For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997

Section .510(a) of OMB Circular A-133 requires the auditor to report as audit findings:
(1) reportable conditions, (2) material noncompliance, (3) known or likely questioned costs
exceeding $10,000 for a type of compliance requirement within a major Federal awards
program, (4) known questioned costs exceeding $10,000 for a Federal awards program not
audited as a major program, (5) known fraud affecting a Federal awards program unless
otherwise reported outside this auditor’s report pursuant to the direct reporting requirements of
Government Auditing Standards, (6) instances where the results of audit follow-up procedures
disclose that the summary schedule of prior audit findings prepared by the auditee materially
misrepresents the status of any prior audit finding, and (7) the circumstances concerning why
the auditor’s report on compliance for major programs is other than an unqualified opinion,
unless such circumstances are otherwise reported as audit findings under one of the preceding
categories. In addition, Section .500(e) of OMB Circular A-133 requires the auditor to follow
up on prior audit findings regardless of whether a prior audit finding relates to a major
program in the current year. Accordingly, instances in which prior audit findings have not
been corrected will also be reported as audit findings in this report, regardless of whether the

prior audit findings pertained to a program audited as a major program in the current year.

This part presents our audit findings pursuant to the above-noted requirements. The
findings are presented in the following schedules based on the three basic types of entities

administering the respective Federal awards programs:

EXCLUDING THE STATE UNIVERSITY SYSTEM AND THE STATE
COMMUNITY COLLEGE SYSTEM

e SCHEDULE II - FINDINGS AND QUESTIONED COSTS - THE STATE
UNIVERSITY SYSTEM AND THE STATE COMMUNITY COLLEGE SYSTEM

UNITED STATES DEPARTMENT OF AGRICULTURE

Food Distribution Program (CFDA
No. 10.550; State Agency, FDACS)

Special Tests and Provisions. The provisions of Title 7, Section 250.30(n)(1), Code of

Federal Regulations, requires the FDACS to monitor processor inventories to ensure that the
quantity of donated foods for which a processor is accountable is the lowest cost-efficient level
but is in no event more than a six-month supply based on the processor’s average monthly
usage, unless a higher level has been specifically approved on the basis of a written
justification submitted by the processor. In audit report No. 12983, paragraph 5, we reported
that the FDACS had not established procedures to ensure compliance with this Federal
regulation. However, in that report we noted that the FDACS had recently established
procedures (i.e., effective January 6 and February 14, 1997) indicating that processors’
inventory levels would be reviewed on the December and June monthly performance reports.
In its Summary Schedule of Prior Audit Findings, dated November 19, 1997, the FDACS
indicated that this prior audit finding had been partially corrected and that implementation of
the procedures noted above could not be verified until February 1998. Furthermore, in its
March 24, 1998, response to our follow-up inquiry during our current audit, the FDACS
indicated that the initial implementation of those procedures was for the first six-month period
of the 1997-98 fiscal year (i.e., subsequent to our audit period). We will review the adequacy

of those procedures in our subsequent audits of the FDACS.

Food Stamp Programs Cluster (CFDA Nos.
10.551 and 10.561; State Agency, FDCFS)

award or other cost objective should be supported by personnel activity reports or equivalent

documentation. That documentation is to be used to equitably allocate those salaries and wages

to the benefiting programs and/or cost objectives. That Section also provides that salaries of

e In addition to the Food Stamp Program, the duties and responsibilities of 4 of these
employees related to the Medical Assistance Program (CFDA No. 93.778), the Family

the Temporary Assistance for Needy Families Program (CFDA No. 93.558). However,
100 percent of the salaries paid to these employees during the sampled pay periods was
charged to the Food Stamp Program. Contrary to the provisions of OMB Circular A-87,
the FDCFS did not maintain time distribution records for these employees. In response
to our inquiry, the FDCFS estimated that these employees spent from 40 to 75 percent of
their time working on Food Stamp Program activities. Based on that estimate and the
salaries charged 100 percent to the Food Stamp Program during the 1996-97 fiscal year,
the Food Stamp Program was overcharged $44,727, of which $22,363 was funded from
Federal funds. (Federal Grant Nos. 3F6402 and 3F7402) A similar finding was
reported in audit report No. 12983, paragraph 14.

e Contrary to the requirements of OMB Circular A-87, periodic certifications were not
obtained by the FDCFS for the remaining 9 employees for which the employees asserted
that they worked solely on the Food Stamp Program. The salaries charged to the Food
Stamp Program during the 1996-97 fiscal year and paid to these 9 employees totaled
$170,324.32.

Because of the above-described deficiencies, the FDCFS system of internal control was not
operating effectively to ensure that only allowable and appropriate salary costs were charged to
the Food Stamp Program. Salary costs charged directly (i.e., not allocated) to the Food Stamp
Program by the FDCFS during the 1996-97 fiscal year totaled $5,651,592. We recommend
that the FDCFS reimburse the Food Stamp Program for the improperly charged salary costs
identified above. We also recommend that those positions relating to the Food Stamp Program
be reviewed to ensure that applicable salary costs are equitably allocated among benefiting

programs and activities. Additionally, time records required by governing regulations should

be maintained.

Activities Allowed or Unallowed - DPAF Investigations. The Auditor General’s Division of

Public Assistance Fraud, working closely with FDCFS, each year conducts a substantial
number of investigations of possible public assistance fraud relating to the Food Stamp
Program, Family Support Payments to States — Assistance Payments Program (AFDC) (and its
replacement program, the TANF Program), and Medical Assistance Program. As disclosed in
audit report No. 13060, covering the 12-month period ended June 30, 1997, the Division
reviewed and disposed of 39,774 cases of suspected public assistance fraud. Of these cases,
36,071 were postfraud (after benefits were paid) and 3,673 were fraud prevention (before
benefits were paid). The 36,071 postfraud cases produced 9,785 completed field
investigations, for which 2,144 cases (program violations), with an estimated fraud amount of
$4,172,150, were referred to appropriate State Attorneys for consideration. Of the 2,144
referred program violations, 981 involved the apparent illegal receipt of food stamps with an
estimated total value of $1,781,439. In addition to the program violations referred to the State
Attorneys, the Division referred 5,441 program violations to the FDCFS for Administrative
Disqualification Hearings pursuant to Title 7, Section 273.16, Code of Federal Regulations.
Administrative Disqualification Hearings during the 1996-97 State fiscal year, regarding cases

referred by the Division during that and prior fiscal years, resulted in dispositions in which

Eligibility. In accordance with controlling Federal regulations, the FDCFS established quality
control units that reviewed the accuracy and propriety of FDCFS eligibility determinations and
benefit payments. Based on these reviews, payment error rates are determined. Payment
errors include, among other things, the issuance of food stamps to ineligible individuals and
the overissuance of food stamps to eligible households. Pursuant to Title 7, Section 2025,
United States Code, a liability will be incurred by a State for any year in which the State’s
payment error rate exceeds the national payment error tolerance level established by the United

States Department of Agriculture (USDA) for that year.

As discussed in audit report No. 12983, paragraph 18, the State of Florida settled the liability
for the 1992 and 1993 Federal fiscal years in a settlement agreement, dated January 13, 1994,
between the FDCFS and the USDA. The January 13, 1994, settlement agreement provided
that all penalties (i.e., liabilities) associated with the 1992 and 1993 Federal fiscal years would
be settled through a series of reinvestments by the State in the Food Stamp Program,
supplemental investments by the USDA Food and Consumer Service (FCS) in the State’s
reinvestment activities, and direct payments by the State to the FCS over a five-year period as
set forth in the agreement. For the 1996-97 State fiscal year, the State was required to reinvest
$3,000,000 in certain activities intended to reduce the food stamp payment error rate. Our
review indicated that FDCFS had expended $5,894,531 for the required activities in the
1996-97 fiscal year. The reinvestment requirement for the 1997-98 State fiscal year is to be

based on payment error rates of subsequent Federal fiscal years.

As noted in audit report No. 12983, paragraph 19, the State’s payment error rates for the 1994
and 1995 Federal fiscal years were 13.59 percent and 11.07 percent, respectively. These rates

exceeded the national payment tolerance levels of 10.32 percent and 9.72 percent for those two

years. During our current audit we noted that correspondence to the State from the USDA
dated June 27, 1997, indicated that for the 1996 Federal fiscal year, the Food Stamp payment
error rate for the State of Florida was 9.70 percent. The correspondence provided that as this
rate exceeded the national payment error tolerance level of 9.22 percent, the State faced a
potential sanction of $323,741. However, the State entered into a settlement agreement with
the USDA on May 1, 1997, which settled the food stamp liabilities for the 1994, 1995, and
1996 Federal fiscal years. That settkement agreement requires, among other things, that the
State reinvest specific amounts in subsequent years in activities designed to improve payment
accuracy. The amounts that must be reinvested depend, in part, on the error rates occurring in
subsequent years. For the 1997 Federal fiscal year, the USDA had not published the national
payment tolerance level or provided the official State of Florida payment error rate as of
March 10, 1998. However, according to FDCFS records, the State’s preliminary estimated
Food Stamp payment error rate for the 1997 Federal fiscal year is 9.60 percent. This estimate
represents a decrease from the 1996 Federal fiscal year official error rate noted above. A
determination cannot be made as to whether the State will be subject to a liability for the 1997
Federal fiscal year until the official tolerance level and State payment error rates are published

by the USDA. We will continue to review the effectiveness of the State’s efforts to reduce

payment error rates in subsequent audits.

amounts reported on the FCS-209 reports for the 1996-97 State fiscal year were obtained from
reports generated from the FDCFS Benefit Recovery System (BRS). We noted that the
FDCFS did not reconcile cash collections reported as reimbursements for food stamp

overissuances on the FCS-209 (i.e., per the BRS) to the SAMAS. The SAMAS is the State’s

Special Tests and Provisions - Case Files. Title 7, Section 272.10, Code of Federal

Regulations, requires State agencies to automate their Food Stamp Program operations and
computerize their systems for obtaining, maintaining, utilizing, and transmitting information
concerning the Food Stamp Program. This includes: (1) processing and storing all case file
information necessary for eligibility determination and benefit calculation, identifying specific
elements that affect eligibility, and notifying the certification unit of cases requiring notices of
case disposition, adverse action and mass change, and expiration; (2) providing an automatic
cutoff of participation for households which have not been recertified at the end of their
certification period; and (3) generating data necessary to meet Federal issuance and
reconciliation reporting requirements. The FDCFS uses the Florida On-Line Recipient
Integrated Data Access (FLORIDA) System to maintain client-related information, determine

eligibility, calculate benefits, and maintain a history of payments to clients.

As part of our tests of the FLORIDA System, we reviewed FDCFS client case files for
documentation to support the information maintained in the FLORIDA System regarding client
eligibility and benefit levels for 60 sampled issuances totaling $10,025. As described in the

following, our tests disclosed that, for 8 of the 60 case files reviewed, the FDCFS was not able

Special Tests and Provisions - Inventory Levels. Pursuant to Title 7, Section 274.7(a)(1),

Code of Federal Regulations, the inventory levels at coupon issuers and bulk storage points
should not exceed a six-month supply, taking into account coupons on hand and on order. Our
review of 3 coupon issuance sites disclosed one site where coupons on hand on September 18,
1997, exceeded an average six-month supply by approximately $5,900,000. Specifically, the
value of coupons on hand as of September 17, 1997, was $14,765,903: whereas, our
calculations showed the average value issued during a six-month period to be $8,812,620 (a
difference of $5,953,283). To minimize the possible unauthorized use of food coupons, we
recommend that the FDCFS monitor its coupon issuance sites to ensure that excessive

inventories are not maintained.

Special Tests and Provisions. Title 24, Subpart H, Code of Federal Regulations, provides the

regulations for the release of Community Development Block Grant — State’s Program (CDBG)
funds by the State to subrecipients. These regulations provide, among other things, that the
subrecipients must request a release of CDBG funds from the State after complying with other
regulations regarding the dissemination or publishing of a “Notice of Intent” to request release
of CDBG funds from the State. Title 24, Section 58.73, Code of Federal Regulations,
provides that the State (i.e., FDCA) may not approve the request for the release of CDBG

funds before 15 calendar days have elapsed from the time of the receipt of the request or from

the time specified in the notice of intent disseminated by the subrecipient, whichever is later.
This regulation is intended to allow any person or agency a period in which to file an objection
with the State in regard to the planned activity for which the CDBG funds will be used. Our
review of the requests for release of CDBG funds submitted by ten FDCA subrecipients
disclosed three instances whereby the FDCA approved the requests prior to the lapse of the
mandated 15-day waiting period. In these instances the approvals were made 9, 12, and 14
days prior to the end of the respective 15-day periods. Although CDBG funds were not
released prior to the end of the respective 15-day periods in each of these instances, the
premature approvals may have miscommunicated to an interested person or agency that the
period to file an objection was over. We recommend that the FDCA implement appropriate

corrective actions to ensure that requests for the release of funds are not approved prior to the

end of the prescribed periods.

Cash Management. Section 8.6.1 of the 1996-97 fiscal year Cash Management Improvement

Act (CMIA) Agreement provides that, for applicable programs, the State shall be liable for
interest on Federal funds from the date Federal funds are credited to a State account until the
date those funds are paid from that account for program purposes. To determine the State’s
interest liability, clearance patterns are developed for the programs covered by the CMIA
Agreement. Those clearance patterns measure the period of time from receipt of the Federal
funds (credit to the State’s account) to the disbursement of those funds (debit to the State’s
account). Section 6.1.3 of the CMIA Agreement provides that, for direct program and direct
administrative costs, the State agencies should (for most programs) request Federal funds so

that they are received in the State’s account no earlier than the day the corresponding warrant

(drawn) in this manner.

Florida Department of Labor and Employment Security (FDLES) procedures for drawing
Federal funds for Federal programs covered by the CMIA Agreement were based on a daily
cash analysis of applicable FDLES trust funds. This analysis included a calculation of the
excess of expenditures (outstanding vouchers and transfers) over cash (cash balance plus
outstanding deposits). As individual trust funds sometimes included programs covered by the
CMIA and programs not covered by the CMIA, this analysis could not be attributed to only
those specific Federal programs covered by the CMIA. In using this methodology, the FDLES
did not provide for the maintenance of records that correlated, for all costs, the Federal funds
drawn to specific vouchers. Specifically, records correlating Federal funds drawn to specific
vouchers were maintained for payroll costs (for all programs) and for payments to
subrecipients under the Job Training Partnership Act Program (CFDA No. 17.250) and the
ETA - Dislocated Workers Program (CFDA No. 17.246). However, for Federal funds drawn
for other direct and administrative program costs, no such records were maintained. The
major programs impacted by this finding included the Employment Service Program, CFDA
No. 17.207; Unemployment Insurance Program, CFDA No. 17.225; ETA - Dislocated
Workers Program, CFDA No. 17.246; Job Training Partnership Act Program, CFDA No.
17.250; Rehabilitation Services - Vocational Rehabilitation Grants to States Program, CFDA
No. 84.126; and the Social Security - Disability Insurance Program, CFDA No. 96.001.

Similar findings were noted in audit report No. 12983, paragraphs 70 and 71. Absent records
that correlate vouchers to a specific draw, the FDLES has not demonstrated compliance with
the CMIA Agreement. As a result, the FDLES does not have adequate assurance that accurate
information was reported to the State CMIA Coordinator to allow for necessary adjustments to

the applicable clearance patterns in the calculation of the State’s interest liability. To ensure

Employment Service (CFDA No.
17.207; State Agency, FDLES)

Activities Allowed or Unallowed/Allowable Costs. One of the FDLES activities funded by

Employment Service funds is job placement services for eligible individuals. This service is
provided by the FDLES through contracts with organizations that are reimbursed based on the
number of successful job placements. Periodically, these organizations invoice the FDLES for
payment. The contractor invoices indicate the individuals successfully placed and the
respective employers. The invoices are required to be supported by statements (placement
certification forms), signed by both the contractor and the individual receiving services,
attesting that the related employment information is correct (e.g., the individual was retained in
unsubsidized employment for a minimum period). Prior to paying such an invoice, FDLES
procedures require that FDLES employees confirm the validity and accuracy of the information
provided on the placement verification form with the applicable employer. Specifically,
FDLES policy PAD 97-04 provides that a computer-generated employment verification letter
will be produced from the information contained on the placement certification form. The
letter is to be mailed to the employer, with a self-addressed return envelope, for verification of
the information and return to the FDLES. This policy also provides that on-site visits and
telephone contacts may be used to corroborate the employer verification letters. This process
serves as a control to ensure that the contractors are only reimbursed for services rendered in
accordance with contractual terms and conditions. During the 1996-97 fiscal year, the FDLES

reimbursed contractors $2,142,693 for these job-placement services.

As part of our test of 60 charges to the Employment Service Program, we examined 7

payments to contractors for job-placement services relating to 23 individuals. Based on our

test results we elected to review an additional 8 such payments for job-placement services

applicable funds. (Federal Grant No. 92056)

terms and conditions, we recommend that the FDLES take appropriate action to ensure

compliance with established policy regarding verification of job placements.

Equipment and Real Property Management. Title 29, Section 97.32, Code of Federal

Regulations, provides that a state will use, manage, and dispose of equipment acquired under a
USDOL grant in accordance with state laws and procedures. Section 273.02, Florida Statutes,
provides that each item of property which is practicable to identify by marking shall be marked
in the manner prescribed by the Auditor General, and that each custodian (e.g., State agency)
shall maintain an adequate record of property in the custodian’s inventory. Chapter 10.350,
Rules of the Auditor General, requires that custodians maintain adequate records of property in
their custody that contain various information such as the identification and serial number,
description, physical location, acquisition cost, date acquired, and name of custodian. Chapter
10.360, Rules of the Auditor General, also requires that each property item be permanently
marked, if practicable, for the purpose of establishing its identity and ownership by the State

agency holding title to the item.

Our test of 60 charges to the Employment Service Program included 20 nonsalary
administrative expenditures. Two of these 20 expenditures represented acquisitions of
property. One item was a computer printer ($807) acquired in July 1996 and the other was a
computer monitor ($1,377) acquired in December 1996. We noted that, contrary to governing
laws and regulations, these items had not been recorded in the FDLES property records or
marked with a permanent property tag as of July 18, 1997; 1 year (printer) and 6 months
(monitor) after the respective purchases. In response to our inquiry, FDLES indicated that it
estimated these items would be properly recorded and marked by August 31, 1997. (Federal
Grant No. X49195008060) Similar findings were noted in our test of tangible personal
property items acquired by the FDLES in the 1995-96 State fiscal year, as described in audit
report No. 12953, paragraphs 38 through 42. Tangible personal property (i.e., generally,
property items with acquisition costs exceeding $500) purchased and charged by the FDLES to
the Employment Service Program during the 1996-97 State fiscal year totaled $1,778,673. To

Employment and Training Assistance - Dislocated Workers
and Job Training Partnership Act Programs Cluster
(CFDA Nos. 17.246 and 17.250; State Agency, FDLES)

Activities Allowed or Unallowed/Cost Principles. The FDLES’ Division of Jobs and Benefits

(J & B) operates Jobs and Benefits Offices throughout the State for the provision of
employment and benefit services to Florida citizens and employers. In order to provide for an
integrated information management system between the various office locations, the FDLES
contracted with a private vendor in May 1996 to provide computer equipment and software
necessary to establish a network throughout the State. That network is known as the
Workforce Development Wide Area Network (WDWAN). FDLES-administered Federal
programs which participate in the use of the WDWAN include the Employment and Training
Assistance — Dislocated Workers (ETA-DW), CFDA No. 17.246; Job Training Partnership
Act (JTPA), CFDA No. 17.250; Employment Service (ES), CFDA No. 17.207;
Unemployment Insurance (UI), CFDA No. 17.225; and Rehabilitation Services — Vocational
Rehabilitation Grants to States (RS), CFDA No. 84.126 Programs. The Division of J & B’s
service partners, e.g., the Florida Department of Children and Family Services and local

district school boards, also share in the use of the WDWAN.

The cost of computer equipment, software, and related services for the WDWAN totaled
$6,752,344. Of this amount, $6,643,953 was charged to various grants within the ETA-DW
Program. Part of the $6,752,344 costs for the WDWAN were incurred in the acquisition of
1,290 personal computers. Of those 1,290 computers, 196 were located in administrative
offices within the FDLES Office of the Secretary and Administrative Services (OSAS), as

noted in the following:

These offices appear to have functions which are primarily administrative in nature and which
would not be expected to have any responsibilities directly related to the administration of any
particular Federal program. Normally, expenses associated with these FDLES offices are
charged against individual Federal programs through an indirect cost allocation process as
prescribed by the applicable indirect cost agreement with the Federal Government. This cost
allocation process ensures that applicable programs are only charged for their proportionate
share of total costs. However, contrary to this concept, 155 of the 196 computers were
charged directly to the ETA-DW Program. The cost of these 155 computers totaled

approximately $330,400. The costs of the remaining 41 computers were charged to the OSAS.

In correspondence dated January 26, 1998, we requested FDLES personnel to provide an
explanation as to why these 155 computers were purchased and charged to a Federal program
within the Division of J & B (i.e., charged to the ETA-DW Program), but were delivered to
and being used by the OSAS offices. In their response, dated February 6, 1998, FDLES staff
indicated that the OSAS offices using the computers provided direct administrative support
services and assistance to the Division of J & B and, therefore, the equipment used within

those offices was also considered as used to provide direct support to the J & B programs. In
