response to our subsequent request for further clarification of this matter, FDLES staff
indicated, in correspondence dated April 10, 1998, that the implementation of the WOWAN
had increased the workload on FDLES entities (the OSAS offices described above) that support
the Division of J & B over and beyond that which is provided to other divisions within the

FDLES.

The use of equipment acquired from funds of a specific Federal program for areas within the
FDLES that provide indirect support to several FDLES programs/functions appears to
circumvent the intent of standard Federal cost principles (i.e., costs should be allocated to
ensure that all benefiting areas share in those costs in an equitable manner). We recommend
that the FDLES determine the total costs of the computers, monitors, and any other related
software and services used by the OSAS and charged directly to the ETA-DW Program. After
that determination, those costs should be credited to the ETA-DW Program and then allocated
to all benefiting areas (e.g., programs, offices, functions, etc.) in accordance with standard

Federal cost principles.

In our March 27, 1998, correspondence, we also requested that FDLES personnel describe
how programs (see paragraph 25) other than the ETA-DW Program, which share in the use of
the WDWAN, participated in the costs of implementing and operating the WDOWAN. The
Director of the Division of J & B responded that all programs that are operated by the Division
of J & B pay their fair (equitable) share of the cost of the WDWAN by participating in the
recurring costs that average between $400 and $600 per month for each office site.
Notwithstanding that other programs participate in those monthly recurring (i.e., operating)
costs, FDLES personnel acknowledged that they are not aware of any contributions from other
FDLES-administered Federal programs for the development and implementation costs of the
WDWAN. We recommend that the FDLES determine the appropriate costs (hardware,
software, and any other appropriate items) applicable to the development and implementation

of WDWAN. Upon the determination of those costs, the FDLES should credit the ETA-DW

Reporting Requirements. The FDLES is required to submit various reports to the United

States Department of Labor, Employment and Training Administration (USDOL, ETA) for
State activity relating to the Job Training Partnership Act (CFDA No. 17.250) and the ETA -
Dislocated Workers (CFDA No. 17.246) Programs. These reports include, among others, the
JTPA Annual Service Delivery Area Report (ETA 9045), the Worker Adjustment Annual SSA
Report (ETA 9046), and the JTPA Title Quarterly Status Report (ETA 9040). As noted in the
following, our review disclosed that these reports were not always accurately prepared for

submission to the USDOL:

e The ETA 9045 report provides the USDOL annual data on program participant levels by
service delivery area (SDA), as well as the amount of program funds available to and
expended by each SDA within the State. Our examination of the ETA 9045 for the
report period ended June 30, 1996, disclosed fund availability and expenditures were

incorrectly reported for 22 of the State’s 25 SDAs. Specifically:

- For the Title I-A program component, the ETA 9045 reported funds totaling
$51,259,267 available to all of the State's 25 SDAs. We noted that the individual
available fund amounts reported for 21 SDAs were incorrect. The errors ranged
from a $211,809 understatement to a $2,397,513 overstatement. The net effect of
all errors was an overstatement of $3,424,266. Similarly, the report reflected
expenditures totaling $42,038,122 for the State’s 25 SDAs. We noted that the
individual expenditure amounts reported for 20 SDAs were incorrect. The errors

ranged from an understatement of $1,164,249 to an overstatement of $2,320,914.

No. 17.246) for any program year, not less than 50 percent shall be expended for retraining

Furthermore, Title 20, Section 631.14(c), Code of Federal Regulations, requires that of the

Code of Federal Regulations, provides that the funding period is the three-year period that the
funds are available for expenditure as provided in Section 161(b) of the Job Training and
Partnership Act. Title 20, Section 627.455(d)(3), Code of Federal Regulations, requires that
the final financial report (i.e., Workers Adjustment Formula Financial Report, ETA 9041) be
submitted 90 days after the expiration of the funding period. Similarly, Title 20, Section
627.485, Code of Federal Regulations, provides for the timely closeout of each grant and
specifies that revisions to reported expenditures for the program year (i.e., annual grant) may
be made until 90 days after the end of the funding period. Extensions of the 90-day period
may be approved if the State agency makes a written request with justification. After that
time, all reports are considered as final and no additional revisions may be made.
Accordingly, the 1992 program year funding period ended June 30, 1995, and the 1993
program year funding period ended June 30, 1996. Without Federally-approved extensions,
the final financial reports were, therefore, due September 28, 1995, for the 1992 program year

and September 28, 1996, for the 1993 program year.

Opinion Qualification. Because of the lack of adequate support for the computer equipment

and related charges to the ETA-DW Program as noted in paragraphs 25 through 30, the
FDLES system of internal control was not operating in a manner to ensure program funds were
used materially in compliance with program regulations. Additionally, because of the
reporting deficiencies noted in paragraphs 31 through 37 above, the FDLES system of internal
control was not operating in a manner to ensure the accurate reporting of information relative
to program activities and functions. The accurate reporting of that information is necessary to

demonstrate material compliance with applicable program regulations.

(CFDA No. 20.205; State Agency, FDOT)

Activities Allowed or Unallowed/Cost Principles - Overbillings. In the administration of

Federally-funded projects, the FDOT is responsible for processing expenditure transactions in
such a manner as to ensure that specific costs are identified as being either Federally
participating or nonparticipating (i.e., either eligible or ineligible for Federal reimbursement).
In order to accomplish this, FDOT procedures provide that when transactions are entered into
the accounting records, a code will be assigned which designates them as being either
Federally participating or Federally nonparticipating. Federal regulations provide specific
criteria for such costs to be considered for Federal participation. Our review of FDOT records
disclosed the following instances in which the FDOT improperly identified costs as

participating or otherwise overbilled the FHWA:

e Title 23, Section 630.305, Code of Federal Regulations (subsequently Title 23, Section
635.127, Code of Federal Regulations, effective April 1, 1997), provides that liquidated
damages shall be assessed by means of deductions, for each calendar day or workday
overrun in contract time, from the payments otherwise due to the contractor for
performance in accordance with the contract terms. The FHWA is to be credited for its
share of the liquidated damages. During our review of FDOT records, we noted one
instance where the FDOT did not credit the FHWA for the full amount of liquidated
damages. In this instance, FDOT records indicated that liquidated damages in the amount
of $161,291.35 were incorrectly coded as a Federally nonparticipating item. Subsequent
to our audit inquiry, FDOT personnel credited the FHWA for $161,291.35 of liquidated
damages. (Federal Project No. 315-6176-007)

e Title 23, Section 635.121(b), Code of Federal Regulations, provides that contract time
extensions granted by the FDOT shall be subject to the concurrence of the FHWA
Division Administrator and will be considered in determining the amount of Federal
participation. As noted above, Title 23, Section 630.305(a), Code of Federal
Regulations (subsequently Title 23, Section 635.127, Code of Federal Regulations,
effective April 1, 1997), provides that liquidated damages shall be assessed by means of
deductions, for each calendar day or workday overrun in contract time (e.g., days
beyond authorized contract time extensions), from the payments otherwise due to the
contractor for performance in accordance with the contract terms. Should the FDOT
extend the number of contract days without Federal participation in the additional days
extended, the FHWA will continue to fund the project up to the authorized amount.
However, the Federal portion of the project must be credited for liquidated damages
applicable to the excess period regardless of whether the FDOT has authorized the
extension and does not intend to charge the contractor for the liquidated damages. The
FDOT Office of Construction, Construction Project Administration Manual (CPAM),
Chapter 4, Section 1, Item D.3., states that after the project is complete and the FDOT
Final Estimates Office has completed their check (including final time and Construction
Engineering and Inspection costs), they will record on their modified form the number of
days and the dollar amount that will be Federal-aid nonparticipating. A copy of this
form is sent to the FDOT Federal-aid Office with the final project cost data to assist the
Federal-aid Office in the determination of the final amounts that should be credited to the

FHWA for liquidated damages.

As similarly noted in audit report No. 12983, paragraphs 137 through 139, our review of
a Federally-funded contract with a time overrun disclosed that the number of days with
Federal participation was not properly computed. Specifically, on Federal Project No.

Title 23, Section 140.505, Code of Federal Regulations, identifies the conditions under
which administrative settlement costs in defense of contract claims on Federally
participating State projects are eligible for Federal reimbursement. Our review of all
arbitration settlement expenditures coded as Federally participating disclosed one
instance in which $375 was improperly billed to the FHWA. This overcharge occurred
when the FDOT claimed reimbursement for an arbitration settlement expenditure without
obtaining FHWA approval. Subsequent to our inquiry, the FDOT corrected this
overcharge with a credit to the FWHA for $375. (Federal Project No. 118-4602-006)

Title 23, Section 140.713, Code of Federal Regulations, states that general
administration, supervision, and other overhead costs considered necessary for the
management, supervision, and administrative control of a suitably equipped, staffed, and
operational State Highway Agency are unallowable. Our review of all FDOT division
overhead expenditures coded as Federally participating disclosed one transaction totaling
$127.77 that was improperly billed to the FHWA. In this instance, travel costs were
appropriately charged as an allowable direct cost to the FHWA when paid. However,
prior to the date the FDOT reimbursed the employee for the travel costs, the costs were
improperly coded as overhead costs and charged to the FHWA as a payable. Although
the actual costs were allowable charges to the program, the FHWA was improperly

billed for these costs twice (i.e., once as allowable direct costs and once as unallowable

Activities Allowed or Unallowed/Cost Principles - State-Owned Land Exchanges. Title 23,

Section 710.304, Code of Federal Regulations, provides that when State-owned lands are
exchanged for lands required for highway purposes, Federal funds may participate in the
current fair market value of the land being exchanged. Participation in the total consideration
may not exceed the pro rata share of the fair market value of the land required for highway
purposes plus damages. During the audit period, the FDOT made one land exchange in which
two State-owned parcels with settlement values totaling $564,600 and State funds of $94,500
were exchanged for four right-of-way parcels with settlement values totaling $659,100. Three
of the four right-of-way parcels were acquired for use in a Federally-funded project. In our

review of this exchange, we noted that the FDOT allocated the amount of the cash payment

payment and the remaining parcel utilized in the nonFederally-funded project was allocated the

relative values of the four parcels would appear to be the intent of the noted Federal regulation.
The allocation of the cash payment, on a basis other than relative settlement values, resulted in
the FDOT overbilling the FHWA by $26,978. (Federal Project No. OAV-4852-085) We
recommend that the FDOT implement a procedure to ensure that subsequent allocations of this

nature are based on relative values of the parcels acquired. Additionally, the FDOT should

credit the applicable Federal project for the amounts overbilled.

Reporting. Title 49, Section 18.41(b)(3), Code of Federal Regulations, states that the Federal
agency may prescribe the frequency of financial reports for each project or program. A final
voucher report is prepared for each Federal project to notify the FHWA of project completion
and summarize costs incurred. At the completion of each project, any remaining unexpended
Federal funds are released for use on other projects or, if the amount of reimbursable costs at
project completion exceeds the amount obligated in the Federal project agreement, a
modification agreement is prepared to request additional funding for the project.
Correspondence from the FHWA indicates that Federal-aid projects should be final vouchered

within six months of project completion.

FDOT procedures state that the FHWA considers a project complete after the FHWA has
issued a Final Acceptance Report. However, the FHWA only issues Final Acceptance Reports

for full FHWA oversight construction projects. Final Acceptance Reports are generally not

Subrecipient Monitoring. Pursuant to the provisions of the OMB circulars implementing the
Single Audit Act (both the initial and revised acts), pass-through entities (e.g., the FDOT)
must ensure that applicable subrecipients meet the audit requirements of the Single Audit Act
and submit the resulting audit reports to the pass-through entities. The pass-through entities
are to use such reports to assist in the determination of whether the subrecipients expended
Federal funds, provided by the pass-through entities, in accordance with governing laws,
regulations, and applicable provisions of grant and contractual agreements. In accordance with
these requirements, the FDOT has established procedures providing that the respective FDOT
program offices obtain and review audit reports for those subrecipients receiving Federal funds
from the FDOT. These procedures require that the applicable FDOT offices document the
review of the audit reports, document that the reports were timely received, and follow up on
any compliance issues, questioned costs, or internal control deficiencies relating to FDOT-

funded programs.

As part of our audit, we reviewed the FDOT’s review of ten subrecipient audit reports that
were due to the FDOT during the 1996-97 State fiscal year. These ten subrecipients
represented FDOT subrecipients that received at least $300,000 of Federal funds, including

some Highway Planning and Construction Program funds, from the FDOT during the

respective audit periods. For three of the ten subrecipients, the FDOT did not provide

For two of those three reports, the FDOT also did not document that the reports were timely
received. In response to our inquiry, the FDOT indicated that the applicable FDOT office did

were received. However, they indicated that reviews of subrecipient audit reports received
subsequent to that period were being documented. To ensure that Federal funds are being

properly administered by subrecipients, we recommend that the FDOT document the receipt

and review of subrecipient audit reports.

UNITED STATES DEPARTMENT OF THE TREASURY

General (State Agency, FDBF)

Cash Management. One of the funding techniques elected by the State in its 1996-97 fiscal

year Cash Management Improvement Act (CMIA) Agreement with the United States Treasury
was preissuance funding. This funding technique is a method of transferring Federal funds to
a state prior to the date the state issues a warrant or initiates an electronic fund transfer (EFT)
payment. Under this funding technique, the state incurs an interest liability to the Federal
Government from the day Federal funds are credited to a state account to the day the state pays
out the funds for purposes of the applicable Federal programs. Title 31, Section 205.8(a),
Code of Federal Regulations, provides that when required by a funding technique, a clearance
pattern will be used to schedule the transfer of funds to a state and to support the calculation of
interest. For example, if a clearance pattern indicates that it takes three days for a warrant to
"clear" the state's bank account (i.e., the state's bank account is charged) after the warrant is
issued, and the Federal funds were transferred and credited to the state's bank account two
days prior to the date the warrant was issued, then the interest liability would be calculated by

the Florida Department of Insurance (State Coordinator) on a total of five days. Title 31,

Section 205.8(b), Code of Federal Regulations, requires a state to ensure that a clearance
pattern accurately represents the flow of Federal funds and reflects seasonal or other periodic
variations in Clearance activity. Title 31, Section 205.8(d), Code of Federal Regulations, and
the CMIA Agreement provide that an authorized State official shall re-certify the accuracy of
the clearance pattern at least every five years. The CMIA Agreement for the 1996-97 fiscal
year provided for the use of average clearance patterns in connection with the preissuance

funding technique. According to the CMIA Agreement, the Florida Department of Banking

and Finance (FDBF) was responsible for the development of the average clearance patterns.

Average clearance patterns are based on data maintained within the accounting records of the
State and the State's fiscal agents. The CMIA Agreement required the State agencies to
request Federal funds for direct program and direct administrative costs so that they were
received in the State's account no earlier than the day the warrant or EFT request vouchers
were presented to the FDBF, Office of the Comptroller, for voucher audit. For payroll costs,
State agencies were to request Federal funds so that they were received no earlier than the
voucher date per the published payroll schedule. The CMIA Agreement also provided that in
the development of clearance patterns all State warrants and EFT transactions pertaining to
direct program, administrative, and payroll costs of applicable programs were to be analyzed
for a period of time not less than three consecutive months. For each warrant and EFT
transaction selected for analysis, the FDBF was to use the date that the related warrant or EFT
request voucher was submitted to voucher audit (referred to in SAMAS as the "Comptroller
Received Date") and the date that the funds were debited from the State's account to determine

the clearance pattern.

Our review disclosed that the State materially complied with governing regulations in the
development of the average clearance patterns for the 1996-97 fiscal year. However, our
testing disclosed that selected data contained on the CMIA Data File, the composite of

warrants and EFT transactions analyzed by the FDBF in developing the clearance patterns, was

Treatment of Substance Abuse Program, CFDA No. 93.959; and the Social Security -
Disability Insurance Program, CFDA No. 96.001.) The FDBF also indicated that this error
may have existed in the clearance patterns established and used in the prior year (i.e., 1995-96
fiscal year). As a result, the State CMIA Coordinator indicated that for those clearance patterns
affected by the erroneous data, clearance patterns from the 1994-95 fiscal year will be
re-certified and used in the calculation of the State’s interest liability for the 1996-97 fiscal
year. Furthermore, if it is determined that the 1995-96 fiscal year clearance patterns were also
in error, the State CMIA Coordinator indicated that the interest liability for that year will be
recalculated using the corrected data. The recertification of a prior year clearance pattern
appears to be in accordance with Title 31, Section 205.8(d), Code of Federal Regulations, and
the CMIA Agreement. On December 23, 1997, the State CMIA Coordinator notified the
United States Treasury, Federal Management Service, of its plan to use a prior year clearance

pattern in the State’s 1996-97 fiscal year interest liability calculation, and to recalculate the

1995-96 fiscal year interest liability if necessary.

While we recognize that the State’s use of prior year clearance patterns is allowable under the
CMIA, it appears that if this error had not been brought to the attention of FDOI and FDBF
management through our audit inquiries, incorrect clearance patterns would have been certified
and used in the interest liability determination for the 1996-97 fiscal year. We recommend that
the FDBF take the necessary steps to ensure that production job control language is accurate,

complete, and identical to that tested.

must ensure that applicable subrecipients meet the audit requirements of the Single Audit Act
and submit the resulting audit reports to the pass-through entities. The pass-through entities

document the review of the audit reports, and follow up on any compliance issues, questioned

costs, or internal control deficiencies relating to FDCA-funded programs.

As a part of our review, we selected a sample of 14 FDCA subrecipients that were awarded
FEMA Disaster Assistance Program funds and that submitted or were required to submit audit
reports to the FDCA during the period June 30, 1997, through October 31, 1997. These
subrecipients represented the FDCA subrecipients that received the most Disaster Assistance
Program funds during the three-year period July 1, 1994, through June 30, 1997. Each of
these 14 subrecipients received in excess of $300,000 during either the 1995-96 or the 1996-97
State fiscal year. Our review disclosed that the FDCA had not obtained the required audit
reports for 3 of the 14 subrecipients as of January 8, 1998. At that date, these reports were
overdue for periods ranging from two to six months. For these subrecipients, we noted that
the FDCA had not made appropriate efforts to obtain the overdue reports. In response to our
inquiry on January 8, 1998, the FDCA requested (on January 29, 1998) the applicable

subrecipients to provide the overdue reports.

reviewed by FDCA staff.

UNITED STATES DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION

General (State Agency, FDOE)

Allowable Costs/Cost Principles. Under the FDOE Personnel Activity Reporting System

(PARS), which was implemented in July 1996, the FDOE is to assign estimated percentages of
employees’ salaries to accounts representative of various funding sources and, if necessary, is
to adjust those estimates based on actual time records from specified months. Incorporated
within the PARS is the PAR form which is the mechanism by which FDOE employees will
document the actual time spent on individual grant programs or cost objectives. The form is
required to be completed by employees whose salaries are funded, in full or in part, from

Federal funds or are included within a Federal cost-sharing (or matching) obligation. The

PAR form includes the employee’s name, position number, reporting month, and EFFORT
paragraph or statement. The EFFORT statement is to provide the employee with a brief
description of the advance allocation of the employee’s time and effort among various program
objectives. The form is required only during designated reporting months (e.g., September,
January, and May for the 1996-97 fiscal year). The time and effort expended by employees on
supervisory and clerical activities is allocated based on the average time and effort of the
employees supervised or the employees to whom the clerical position provides support. In
addition, a “team allocation approach” is used for some employees, whereby salary costs of

team members are apportioned to Federal programs based on the funding contributed by such

programs to authorized team activities.

Our review of the FDOE efforts with regard to the September reporting period were noted in
audit report No. 12983, paragraphs 222 through 229. Audit tests for the 1996-97 fiscal year
included an evaluation of FDOE procedures for the January and May reporting periods and a
review of FDOE records relative to 53 of the approximate 360 applicable FDOE employees.

sample. The supervisors indicated that the work of two of these three OPS employees
included programs and/or activities that did not qualify for Federal funding. However,
in both cases, we noted that 100 percent of the employees’ salary costs were charged to

Federal programs.

employee’s time and effort pertained to supervising and directing staff. Therefore,

Salary costs charged by the FDOE to major Federal programs during the audit period totaled
approximately $8,500,000. In an effort to ensure that future salary costs are equitably charged
to applicable Federal programs and cost objectives, we recommend that the FDOE continue its
efforts to allocate salary costs based on actual, documented employee time and efforts in the

manner prescribed by established FDOE procedures.

Cash Management. Title 34, Section 80.37(a)(4), Code of Federal Regulations, provides that

when awarding and administering subgrants, States shall conform any advances of grant funds
to subgrantees substantially to the same standards of timing and amount that apply to cash
advances by Federal agencies. Chapter 5 of the Recipient’s Guide for the United States
Department of Education Payment Management System (EDPMS) provides that, based on
United States Department of Treasury regulations, recipient organization responsibilities
include establishing a financial management system to ensure the requesting of funds as needed
for immediate disbursement (whenever possible) and disbursing funds timely, usually within
three business days. Additionally, recipient organizations are responsible for monitoring the

funds held by secondary recipients to ensure effective management of Federal funds.

For the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 1997
During the period July 1, 1996, to June 25, 1997, FDOE personnel advanced cash to
subrecipients twice each month. FDOE personnel had established a monthly cash allowance
for each subrecipient by dividing the subrecipient’s prior year expenditures by ten. Prior to
the second distribution of cash to a subrecipient for a month, FDOE personnel computed the
subrecipient’s adjusted cash balance by obtaining the subrecipient’s cash balance as recorded
on the current FDOE Distributive Aid Report and adjusted that amount by the average prior

monthly expenditures. If the adjusted cash balance was less than or equal to the monthly

allowance, FDOE procedures generally provided for an advance to be made.

As similarly noted in several previous audit reports, most recently in audit report No. 12983,
paragraphs 206 through 209, it was not apparent how the FDOE procedures met the
requirements imposed by the Federal agencies on recipient agencies, in that they allowed
subrecipients to maintain cash to meet expenditures in excess of immediate needs, i.e., cash
was advanced to cover more than a three-day period of expenditures. Our review of 17 audit
reports for District School Boards (DSBs) for the fiscal year ended June 30, 1997, disclosed
that the total available cash for 1 DSB significantly exceeded cash needs. Excess cash for the

DSB ranged from a low of $113,052 to a high of $170,800.

Effective June 26, 1997, the FDOE amended its cash advance procedures to allow
subrecipients to request funds daily. We will review the effectiveness of these procedures in

precluding subrecipients from maintaining excess cash in our subsequent audits of the FDOE.

Subrecipient Monitoring - Report Timeliness. Title 34, Section 80.40(a), Code of Federal

Regulations, provides that grantees are responsible for managing the day-to-day operations of
grant and subgrant-supported activities. Grantees must monitor grant and subgrant-supported
activities to assure compliance with applicable Federal requirements and that performance
goals are being achieved. Grantee monitoring must cover each program, function, or activity.

In accordance with these regulations, FDOE procedures provide for the monitoring of

subgrantees (e.g., subrecipients), including DSBs. Upon completion of monitoring a DSB,
FDOE procedures provide that a report of noted findings is to be sent to the district. The

districts are responsible for correcting the reported deficiencies.

Our review of FDOE records relating to 15 program monitoring reports issued during the
1996-97 fiscal year disclosed that 3 reports were not timely issued. These 3 reports were
issued for periods ranging from 317 days to 463 days after the respective on-site visits. FDOE
personnel indicated that the delays in issuing 2 of these reports resulted from personnel
changes within the FDOE. Untimely issuance of monitoring reports diminishes the usefulness
of such reports and the FDOE’s ability to ensure that necessary corrective actions are taken in
a timely manner. To increase the usefulness of monitoring reports, we recommend that the
FDOE enhance their procedures to ensure that monitoring reports are timely issued. (The
major programs impacted by this deficiency include the Title I Grants to Local Educational
Agencies [CFDA No. 84.010] and the Special Education - Grants to States [CFDA No.
84.027] Programs.)

Subrecipient Monitoring - Audit Report Follow-Up. Pursuant to the provisions of the OMB

circulars implementing the Single Audit Act (both the initial and revised acts), pass-through
entities (e.g., the FDOE) must ensure that subrecipients meet the applicable audit requirements
of the Single Audit Act and submit the resulting audit reports to the pass-through entities. The
pass-through entities are to use such audit reports in determining whether the subrecipients
expended Federal funds provided by the pass-through entities in accordance with governing
laws, regulations, and applicable provisions of grant and contractual agreements. The
applicable OMB circulars (i.e., OMB Circulars A-128 and A-133) also require the
pass-through entities to ensure that timely and appropriate corrective actions are taken in

regard to the subrecipient audit findings.

In accordance with these requirements, the FDOE has established procedures under which the
FDOE Bureau of Business Services (Bureau) assigns follow up responsibility among
appropriate FDOE personnel for findings noted in audit reports of district school boards (i.e.,
subrecipients of the FDOE). The assignee is provided a copy of the audit finding and written
instructions to follow up on the finding (i.e., to ensure that corrective action is initiated by the

district). The assignee is also instructed to provide a response to the Bureau on the status of

the follow-up efforts and actions within 30 days of receipt of the written instructions.

As part of our audit, we selected a sample of 30 subrecipient audit reports received by the
FDOE during the 1996 calendar year. Follow-up on audit findings noted in those reports
should have been initiated no later than the end of the 1996-97 State fiscal year. Fifteen audit
findings judged to be significant were noted in 10 of these reports. Each of those 10 reports
pertained to a district. Our review of the FDOE’s follow-up efforts for the 15 significant
findings disclosed that procedures were not adequate to ensure that timely corrective actions

were initiated by the applicable districts for 5 of the findings. Specifically:

e For 3 findings, the FDOE assignee provided written recommendations to the applicable
districts for correcting the noted findings. These 3 findings related to inadequate cash
management, untimely financial reports, and failure to make appropriate credits to
Federal programs. However, for 2 of the 3 findings the FDOE did not request or
receive evidence as to the nature of the corrective actions planned, initiated, or
implemented by the applicable districts. For the remaining finding, the FDOE did not
obtain evidence of the corrective action initiated by the applicable district until

subsequent to our audit inquiry.

e For 1 finding relating to inadequate cash management, the FDOE indicated that follow

up was inadvertently not done by FDOE staff.

During the 1996-97 State fiscal year, the FDOE charged expenditures totaling approximately
$4.1 million to the consolidated funds for State Administration. Of that amount, $201,209
represented costs incurred by the FDOE Office of Equal Educational Opportunity. That Office
is responsible for the development of education programs, services, and activities regarding
education equity for the State’s school districts, community colleges, universities, and
state-operated schools. Office functions include, but are not limited to, collecting and
analyzing educational and employment data, providing training and technical assistance,
performing State-level and on-site reviews, and monitoring civil rights compliance. Based on
FDOE records, the Office’s functions appear to be designed to implement or ensure
compliance with various Federal laws and regulations and related implementing State laws and

FDOE regulations, including, for example:

e The Florida Educational Equity Act (Section 228.2001, Florida Statutes, and Chapter
6A-19, Florida Administrative Code).

As part of our testing, we selected and examined 25 expenditures (totaling $66,400) charged to
the consolidated funds for State administration by the FDOE Office of Equal Educational
Opportunity. We noted that 2 of the expenditures were directly related to the programs for
which the administrative funds were consolidated. Specifically, these 2 expenditures
represented travel costs for 2 Office employees to attend a USDOE Regional Conference on
Improving America’s Schools. However, for the remaining 23 expenditures, it was not
apparent that the charges to the consolidated administrative funds were either appropriate
and/or equitable. For example, we noted 1 expenditure ($735) representing travel costs of an
Office employee to attend a conference on civil rights as it relates to vocational education.
That expenditure may have been more appropriately charged to the Vocational Education -
Basic Grants to States Program (CFDA No. 84.048), which is not a program for which
administrative funds were consolidated. Additionally, for the other 22 expenditures
(representing the costs of employee salaries and benefits, subscriptions, computer equipment,
and data processing supplies in the amount of $63,525), documentation or analyses were not

maintained to demonstrate that the costs only benefited the consolidated Federal programs.

Without documentation demonstrating that costs of the FDOE Office of Equal Educational

Reporting Requirements. Each year, a State Educational Agency (SEA) must submit its

average State per pupil expenditure (SPPE) data to the USDOE National Center for Education
Statistics. The SPPE data is used by the USDOE to make allocations under several USDOE
Elementary and Secondary Education Act (ESEA) programs, including the Title I Grants to
Local Educational Agencies (LEAs) Program. SPPE data comprise the State’s annual current
expenditures for free public education, less certain designated exclusions, divided by the
State’s average daily attendance. The SEA’s report is based on data submitted to it by the
LEAs in the State. The SPPE data submitted during our audit period was based on LEA data
for the 1995-96 State fiscal year.

Our examination of the SPPE data submitted by the FDOE on the National Public Education
Financial Survey (NPEFS) indicated that the FDOE generally reported complete and accurate
information. However, as described below, our examination disclosed two instances in which
the FDOE’s procedures for preparing the NPEFS could be improved to provide greater

assurance as to the accuracy of the data reported:

e As defined by Title 20, Section 8801(11), United States Code, the term “current
expenditures” means expenditures for free public education, less certain exclusions.
Pursuant to Title 20, Section 8801(15), United States Code, free public education does
not include education beyond grade 12. Accordingly, expenditures for adult education

programs administered by the LEAs are not to be considered public education

expenditures for the purposes of determining SPPE data. Therefore, when reporting
SPPE data, the FDOE should exclude all LEA adult education program expenditures

Florida Education Finance Program (FEFP) were properly excluded from the SPPE data

program expenditures of approximately $50,000 for one LEA that were not excluded by

the FDOE in calculating the reported State SPPE amount.

The amount expended by LEAs for adult education programs that were not part of the
FEFP was not available from FDOE records. In the absence of that information or
related analyses performed by FDOE personnel, it was not practicable on postaudit to
determine the impact on the reported SPPE amount of $5,499. While the (undetermined)
impact may not materially affect the reported SPPE amount, any impact could potentially

affect the USDOE calculations of subsequent grant awards.

Pursuant to Title 20, Section 8801(1), United States Code, the term “average daily
attendance” means the aggregate number of days of attendance of all students during a
school year, divided by the number of days school is in session during such school year.
Average daily membership (or other similar data) may be converted to average daily
attendance for LEAs in states that provide state aid to LEAs on the basis of average daily
membership or such other data. The FDOE provides aid to LEAs based on the full-time
equivalent (FTE) enrollment of students. The FTE enrollment is determined from four
membership surveys made during a regular 180-day school year. To be eligible for
inclusion on the survey, a student must meet two requirements: (1) the student must be

officially assigned to a course or program for at least one day of the survey week and (2)

the student must have been in attendance at least one of the days of the survey week or
one of the six scheduled days preceding the survey week. Under the FDOE’s
procedures, the FTE enrollment is converted to average daily membership (ADM) and
average daily attendance (ADA) through the use of two conversion factors (.9677 for
ADM and .9292 for ADA). The ADM and ADA calculated under that methodology is
then used to calculate the State’s SPPE amount. According to FDOE personnel, these
conversion factors were developed some years ago (i.e., during the mid 1970’s) when
LEAs were required to report FTE enrollment, ADM, and ADA. Since that time, ADM
and ADA data have not been collected by the FDOE. However, FDOE personnel have
not performed any analyses or other procedures to ensure that the ADM and ADA
conversion factors noted above are still valid. The periodic performance of procedures

to ensure the validity of the ADM and ADA conversion factors is necessary to provide

assurance as to the accuracy of the SPPE data reported on the NPEFS.

We recommend that FDOE personnel enhance the accuracy of the data reported on the NPEFS
by determining, documenting, and excluding expenditures for adult education programs that
are not part of the FEFP. In addition, we recommend that FDOE personnel perform periodic
analyses or other procedures to ensure the validity of ADM and ADA conversion factors used

in calculating the reported SPPE amount.

The FDOE submitted the required child count report to the USDOE by the February 1, 1996,

review of FDOE’s procedures disclosed that FDOE personnel did not, in all instances, ensure

funds to LEAs was properly updated to reflect current child count information. Specifically:

e On May 9, 1996, the FDOE reported to the USDOE 310,184 children with disabilities

children as the actual amount that should have been reported was 310,079 children.

children. That count represented an update (i.e., May 13, 1996) to the count reported to
the USDOE on May 9, 1996. However, the allocation should have been based on the

above-noted correct count of 310,079 children.

These overstated counts reported to the USDOE and used to allocate funds to LEAs apparently
occurred due to a FDOE clerical error and to FDOE personnel inappropriately reporting
December 1994 (i.e., instead of December 1995) counts for six LEAs receiving program funds
pursuant to the State’s Head Start Program. Additionally, the amounts reported on the May 9,
1996, report were not updated for other corrections determined by the FDOE in the May 13,

Reporting Requirements - Loans Records Update and Accuracy. Title 34, Section

682.414(a)(1)(i), Code of Federal Regulations, provides that the guaranty agency shall
maintain current, complete, and accurate records of each loan that it holds. Such records are
to be maintained in a system that allows ready identification of each loan’s current status,
updated at least once every ten business days. This requirement is also applicable to a
third-party servicer that administers any aspect of the FFEL program under a contract with a
guaranty agency, if applicable. Title 34, Section 682.414(a)(1)(li), Code of Federal
Regulations, provides, in part, that the guaranty agency maintain records of all documentation
supporting the claim filed by the lender; notices of changes in a borrower’s address; a payment
history showing the date and amount of each payment received from or on behalf of the
borrower by the guaranty agency; and any additional records that are necessary to document

the accuracy of reports it submits to the USDOE.

The FDOE’s servicing agent receives updated loan information from schools and lenders
through electronic mediums and “hard copy” format (i.e., actual paper documents). The
electronic mediums include (1) updates through the physical submission of data
tapes/disks/files to the servicing agent that must be manually loaded into the servicing agent’s
computer prior to the electronic updating of the database records and (2) real time/on line
updates in which the database records are updated through direct data entry by the schools and
lenders. The FDOE estimated that approximately 93 percent of updates were accomplished
through the real time/on line medium, with the remaining 7 percent accomplished using hard
copy documents and data tapes/disks/files. Our review disclosed that neither the FDOE nor
the servicing agent documented the receipt dates of hard copy documents received for update
purposes from participating schools and lenders. Accordingly, the FDOE could not always

demonstrate that the FDOE loan database was updated within ten business days of receipt of

not include loans categorized in the database as being in grace, forbearance, deferment, or

data obtained from FDOE’s database and that obtained from the applicable lenders, as

As the loan database is the foundation for numerous FDOE reports submitted to the USDOE,
such as the Guaranty Agency Quarterly/Annual Report, it is necessary that the information on
the database be of the highest quality. The quality of the FDOE loan database is contingent
upon current, complete, and accurate loan data. We recognize that the FDOE is to some
degree dependent on lenders and educational institutions for the provision of updated
information regarding loans. We also recognize that FDOE personnel are aware, through
efforts to comply with requirements for submitting data to the National Student Loan Data
System (NSLDS), of instances in which its database may not contain current information.
Notwithstanding these circumstances, FDOE information indicated that as of March 30, 1998,

the database included the following loans for which the lenders of record had not successfully

reported loan information to the FDOE:

e A total of 31,485 RTC loans in “repay” status with outstanding principal of $71,449,213

and

e A total of 155,487 non-RTC loans in “repay” status with outstanding principal of
$42 1,507,872.

The total principal outstanding on these 186,972 loans ($492,957,084) is approximately 30
percent of the total principal outstanding of all loans ($1,665,435,127) recorded in the database

with a “repay” status.

Based on the discrepancies noted during our confirmation process, we recommend that the
FDOE continue its efforts, and enhance existing procedures when possible, to obtain current
information from lenders. We also recommend that the FDOE continue to seek guidance from
the USDOE in establishing procedures to improve the accuracy of the loan database with
regard to those loans made by savings and loan institutions which were liquidated by the RTC

and/or the FDIC.

Reporting Requirements — Quarterly/Annual Report. Title 34, Section 682.414(a)(1)(@i)G),

Code of Federal Regulations, requires the guaranty agency to maintain any records that are
necessary to document its right to receive or retain payments made by the USDOE and the
accuracy of the reports it submits to the USDOE. The guaranty agency reports financial data
related to the operation of the FFEL Program on a monthly, quarterly, and annual basis. The
Guaranty Agency Monthly Claims and Collections Report (ED Form 1189) (Monthly Report)
is used by the guaranty agency to request payments from, and to make payments to, the
USDOE under the FFEL Program. According to reporting instructions issued by the USDOE,
the Monthly Report is the cornerstone of the monthly cycle and is used by the USDOE to carry
out financial activities with the guaranty agency. The form is submitted monthly and reflects
the FFEL activity of the guaranty agency for the loan programs in the previous month. The
USDOE uses the information from the Monthly Report, as well as relevant data from the
Quarterly/Annual Report, to determine and process payments from and to the guaranty agency.
The Guaranty Agency Quarterly/Annual Report (ED Form 1130) (Quarterly/Annual Report) is
required to be submitted by the guaranty agency on both a quarterly and an annual basis. The
information included on the Quarterly/Annual Report is necessary to the USDOE in
determining: (1) if the guaranty agency is properly administering the loan programs under the
FFEL Program; (2) how much to pay each guaranty agency for the administrative cost
allowance (transition support); and (3) the amount of reinsurance fees the guaranty agency
owes the USDOE. In addition, the data reported on the Quarterly/Annual Report is used to

provide information to the Congress and others on various aspects of the FFEL Program.

As part of our audit, we reviewed the FDOE procedures and the records maintained by the
FDOE and its servicing agent that support the September 30, 1996, Quarterly/Annual Report
and the July, August, and September 1996 Monthly Reports. As similarly noted in audit
report No. 12983, paragraph 255, our review disclosed that FDOE’s procedures were not

always adequate to ensure the accuracy and completeness of these reports. Specifically:

Because of the significant reliance placed on the data reported on the Quarterly/Annual Report

by the FDOE in creating the Projections Report (see following paragraphs), it is of utmost
importance that the data contained in the Quarterly/Annual Report be accurate and complete.
As such, we recommend that FDOE, in consultation with its servicing agent, strengthen their

procedures to ensure the proper reporting of data on the Quarterly/Annual Report.

Special Tests and Provisions - Monitoring. Title 34, Section 682.410(c)(1), Code of Federal

Regulations, requires the guaranty agency to conduct comprehensive biennial on-site program
reviews at certain participating lending institutions and schools. Procedures have been
established by the USDOE and the FDOE to ensure that monitoring reports are issued in a
timely manner. The USDOE School Site Review Guide provides that monitoring reports
should be sent to the school within 60 days of the review. The FDOE's procedures for issuing
monitoring reports provide that if the school fails to provide all files, reports or other
information requested on-site, the reviewer will request the missing documents during the exit
interview and follow-up with a written request within 5 working days of the on-site visit. The

school will be allowed 30 days from the exit interview to provide the information. The written

request will also state that if materials are not received by the designated date, the monitoring
report will be issued and the school will be cited for failure to maintain required records. In
cases where the school is genuinely attempting to provide all requested records, but additional
time is justified, the FDOE Program Review Coordinator may grant an extension of time. The

FDOE's procedures further provide that, generally, monitoring reports should be issued 30

days after the receipt of all additional information requested by FDOE personnel.

Our review of FDOE’s monitoring efforts for 16 schools disclosed that FDOE personnel did
not always follow established guidelines and procedures for issuing monitoring reports.
Specifically, our review disclosed seven instances in which FDOE personnel did not issue the
monitoring report within 60 days of review, as required by the USDOE School Site Review
Guide. For these seven instances, the number of days from the date of review to the date of

the monitoring report ranged from 62 days to 336 days, with an average of 179 days.

The untimely issuance of these monitoring reports are attributed to FDOE personnel not
requesting additional information within five days of the exit interview, not receiving
additional information timely (within 30 days of the request of the information or within the
FDOE-granted time extension), and/or not issuing the report within 30 days after the receipt of

all additional information requested by FDOE personnel.

Similar findings were noted in audit report No. 12983, paragraphs 258 through 261. As noted
in that report, the FDOE attributed the delays to gaps in communication between FDOE staff
and school staff, employee turnover, resource limitations, and changes in the loan program. In
response to our current inquiry as to the delays noted above, the FDOE stated that new
monitoring procedures had been finalized in February 1997 and updated in May 1997 and that
each of the noted reviews had commenced prior to the adoption of the new procedures.
Accordingly, the FDOE attributed the delays to the same reasons noted in the prior audit

report. Monitoring reports can be used as a basis for taking limitation, suspension, or

Special Tests and Provisions - Claims Interest. Title 34, Sections 682.404(a)(1) and (a)(2)(i),

Code of Federal Regulations, establishes the provisions for an agreement between the USDOE
and a guaranty agency under which the USDOE reimburses the guaranty agency for losses
related to the amount of unpaid principal and accrued interest on default claims filed by
lenders. As described in a USDOE Dear Colleague Letter (94-G-263), prior to December 20,
1993, certain FFEL Program loans (i.e., Federal Stafford loans meeting certain requirements)
were subject to rebates of excess interest if the interest rates on those loans exceeded a
predetermined comparable market rate. The predetermined comparable market rates were
based on the bond equivalent rates of 91-day Treasury bills. The Higher Education Technical
Amendments of 1993 added Section 427A(i)(7) of the Higher Education Act (Title 20, Section
1077a, United States Code) to require lenders to convert loans subject to rebates of excess
interest to an annual variable interest rate; thereby, such loans will no longer be subject to the
rebate requirements. The interest rate conversion provides benefits to the borrower that are
substantially equivalent to the rebates of excess interest. In October 1994, the USDOE issued
Dear Colleague Letter 94-G-263, which provided guidance to guaranty agencies in
implementing these highly complex changes to the Higher Education Act. Specifically, Section

II and Section IV of that letter provides detailed instructions for interest rate conversion.

As part of our tests we reviewed the FDOE’s calculation of interest paid to lenders on default
claims. These 50 claims included 44 Stafford loans and 6 Supplemental Loans for Students
(SLS) loans. Our review disclosed that the FDOE determinations of interest paid to lenders

were materially correct. However, we noted the following:

e For 1 of the 6 SLS loans tested, the FDOE made an error in assigning the loan to the
appropriate category (under the new regulations) thereby applying an incorrect variable
interest rate. As a result, the FDOE overstated the interest amount for the loan by

$5.46. In response to our inquiries, the FDOE indicated that it would follow up with the

servicing agent to correct the programming error that caused the overstatement.

e For 7 of the 44 Stafford loans tested, the FDOE understated the interest amounts by
$60.04. Correspondence from the FDOE indicated that the FDOE servicing agent’s
system applied the incorrect interest rate caps (i.e., maximum interest rate that can be
charged under variable rates) under the new regulations to certain categories of Stafford
loans subject to variable interest rates. Specifically, loans subject to a 9 percent cap had
a cap of 8.25 percent incorrectly applied in the interest calculation. Correspondence
from the FDOE indicated that on January 22, 1998, the servicing agent completed a
programming correction to the claim file. This correction resulted in adjustments to
8,767 records for a net increase of $52,265 of interest to be paid to lenders on previously

processed default claims.

e For 2 of the 44 Stafford loans tested, interest was initially calculated correctly by FDOE
to be $176.95, but was subsequently adjusted to $373.75. In response to our inquiries
regarding interest adjustments, the FDOE provided an internal memorandum, dated
February 25, 1997, submitted by the FDOE to its servicing agent concerning claims
adjustment increases. This memorandum provided FDOE authorization for the servicing
agent to process a backlog of claim adjustment increases using a $200 tolerance
threshold. In essence, because the difference between the interest claimed by the lenders
and the interest calculated pursuant to program regulations was less than $200 for the 2
noted instances, the lenders were paid the interest amounts claimed. The FDOE did not

provide documentation of its authority to implement a tolerance threshold.

In addition to the effect on the amount of the claim paid and reinsurance requested, the
potential effects of not correctly computing the claim interest results in the borrower’s loan
balance being valued incorrectly when reported to national credit bureaus and collection
agencies. We recommend that the FDOE implement the measures necessary to ensure that
interest claim determinations are accurate and in accordance with applicable Federal
regulations and guidelines. Additionally, in the event that USDOE authority to implement a
tolerance threshold for interest is not provided, the FDOE should determine the amounts of
excess principal paid to lenders by implementing the noted $200 threshold, as well as the

corresponding amount of excess reinsurance that FDOE received from the USDOE, and take

appropriate corrective action to remedy the resulting questioned costs.

Special Tests and Provisions — Collection Charges. Title 34, Section 682.410(b)(2), Code of

Federal Regulations, provides that whether or not provided for in the borrower’s promissory
note, the guaranty agency shall charge a borrower an amount equal to reasonable costs
incurred by the agency in collecting a loan on which the agency has paid a default or
bankruptcy claim. These costs may include, but are not limited to, all attorneys’ fees,
collection agency charges, and court costs. The amount charged a borrower shall equal the
lesser of: (1) the amount that would be charged for the costs of collection under the formula in
Title 34, Section 30.60, Code of Federal Regulations, or (2) the amount the same borrower
would be charged for the cost of collection if the loan was held by the USDOE. This
Regulation was effective February 1993 and is applicable to all loans in default as of the
effective date and not just those loans being placed in collection after the effective date. Our
inquiries of FDOE staff disclosed that the FDOE did not charge borrowers collection costs in

accordance with this regulation. Specifically:

e For defaulted loans processed internally by the FDOE (i.e., not assigned to a collection
agency), the FDOE did not charge the borrowers for collection costs prior to October 1,

1996. Additionally, for loans for which the FDOE paid a default claim on and

to protect the Federal fiscal interest, the Secretary will direct a guaranty agency to promptly

Unless the USDOE Secretary notifies a guaranty agency, in writing, that other loans must be

and any other loans held by the agency for that borrower, have been held by the guaranty
agency for at least four years; for any subsequent fiscal year such loan must have been held by

the agency for at least five years; (3) a payment has not been received on the loan in the last

year; and (4) a judgment has not been entered on the loan against the borrower.

Special Tests and Provisions — Preclaims Assistance. Preclaims assistance (PCA) is the

collection assistance provided to the lender by the guaranty agency in an attempt to prevent a
defaulted loan. Pursuant to Title 34, Section 682.404(a)(2)(1i), Code of Federal Regulations,
PCA involves the initiation by the guaranty agency of at least three collection activities,
including at least one letter to the borrower designed to encourage the borrower to begin or
resume repayment. For a delinquent loan (claim) to be eligible for reimbursement from the
guaranty agency, PCA must have been provided. The lender is required to request PCA

within a specified period subsequent to the loan delinquency date.

As part of the provision of PCA to lenders, the FDOE’s servicing agent’s system generates a
delinquency letter for submission to the borrower once the corresponding loan has been placed
in preclaims status. The servicing agent’s system is also linked to an auto-dialer that
telephones delinquent borrowers for the purpose of encouraging repayment of the loans. The
system records those telephonic collection attempts on the system’s history screens. The first
telephone attempt is made within ten days of receipt of the PCA request from a lender. A total
of four telephonic attempts are made in each subsequent 30-day period. Our review of 60
defaulted and nondefaulted (death and disability) claims disclosed 2 instances in which
delinquent claims, totaling $4,361, were paid to lenders (and reinsured by the USDOE),
although the FDOE did not provide the required preclaims assistance. Specifically, for these
two claims a delinquency letter was sent to the borrower as required. However, there was no
indication that prescribed telephone calls had been made. Correspondence from FDOE
personnel indicated that immediately after the PCA requests had been made, the respective
lenders transferred the underlying loans to other institutions. The FDOE indicated that the

loan transfers precluded the activation of the system’s auto-dialer.

The preclaims assistance process is an important step in averting defaults. We recommend that

the FDOE ensure that preclaims assistance is provided in accordance with governing

Special Tests and Provisions - Timeliness of Claim Payments. Title 34, Section

682.406(a)(8), Code of Federal Regulations, provides that as a condition of receiving
reinsurance, the guaranty agency must pay, or return to the lender for additional
documentation, a default claim within 90 days of the date the lender filed the claim or, if
applicable, the additional documentation. The FDOE utilized a servicing agent to process
lender claims. For this purpose, “process” means that a claim must be fully reviewed and
either returned to the lender for missing documents or approved for payment. To assist the
FDOE in monitoring compliance with the timely processing of the lender claims, the FDOE
obtained from its servicing agent a Claims Pending Adjudication Report which provided the

age of claims for which processing had not been completed.

During the 1996-97 fiscal year, the FDOE paid 25,774 claims totaling $65,267,725. As part
of our tests, we reviewed a sample of 50 of those payments made in the 1996-97 fiscal year by
the FDOE to lenders for defaulted claims. Our review disclosed one claim that was paid 271
days after the lender submitted required additional documentation necessary for the FDOE to
process the claim, or 181 days after the end of the allowed 90-day period. In this instance the
FDOE received reinsurance from the USDOE in the amount of $2,927. In response to our
inquiry, FDOE personnel indicated that this claim had been denied, resulting in the loss of
insurance for the applicable loan, and that the above-cited regulation does not pertain to denied

claims.

In addition to our test, we also analyzed the February 1997 Claims Pending Adjudication
Report and a FDOE report generated and provided to our staff upon audit inquiry. The FDOE
report provided the status of the 311 pending claims included on the February 1997 Claims

Adjudication Report and 121 additional claims that had been assigned a pending status between

the February 1997 report and October 13, 1997. These reports disclosed 341 instances in

these claims in the amount of $670,841.

ranged from 95 to 618 days.

Vocational Education - Basic Grants to States

Allowable Costs/Cost Principles. OMB Circular A-87 provides that costs that benefit more

than one Federal program/cost objective may be allocated to benefiting programs/objectives
pursuant to a cost allocation plan that is approved by the applicable Federal cognizant agency.
During the period July 1, 1996, through September 30, 1996, the FDOE Division of Applied
Technology and Adult Education (Division) incurred nonpayroll administrative costs (e.g.,
equipment and travel) in the amount of $408,242. While the individual charges comprising
these costs pertained to and were initially charged to 13 specific Federal grants/cost objectives
(i.e., as direct costs), we noted that the FDOE reallocated these costs among 8 Federal
grants/cost objectives using allocation rates from the FDOE Personnel Activity Reporting
System (PARS) that were applicable to the Division. The FDOE PARS is an
USDOE-approved system for allocating salaries and wages to Federal programs/cost objectives

based on documented employee time and effort.

The allocation of the Division’s nonpayroll administrative costs was not pursuant to a
Federally-approved cost allocation plan (i.e., the PARS did not address costs other than
salaries and wages). Additionally, based on our analysis of selected charges, the allocations
did not appear appropriate as the costs were initially charged to appropriate benefiting Federal

grant/cost objectives as direct costs. Because of the allocations, the Vocational Education -

is incumbent upon management to establish a system of controls that will enable a
determination of all expenditures which qualify as administrative costs under the provisions of
the Carl D. Perkins Vocational and Applied Technology Education Act and the portion of such
expenditures that are from non-Federal sources; and to accurately report such amounts on
applicable FSRs as a means of demonstrating to the USDOE that the maintenance of effort
requirements have been met. Accordingly, we again recommend that the FDOE assemble and
analyze the expenditure records, project agreements, and/or other relevant documents that will
demonstrate the total amount from the Blueprint for Career Preparation and any other
appropriation categories that was used for providing State administrative functions applicable
to the 1991-92 and subsequent fiscal years. We also recommend that the FDOE, if warranted,

submit revised financial status reports to the USDOE to report all non-Federal outlays for State

Special Tests and Provisions. Title 34, Section 403.13(a)(14), Code of Federal Regulations,

requires subgrants to carry out the Single Parents, Displaced Homemakers, Single Pregnant
Women, and Gender Equity Programs be awarded on a competitive basis. Accordingly,
Section 6.11 of the Florida’s Plan For the Use of Federal Vocational and Applied Technology
Education Funds provided that funds were to be awarded on a competitive basis through the
request-for-proposal (RFP) process. Our review disclosed that the FDOE did not award
Federal Vocational Education Program funds for the Single Parent, Displaced Homemaker,
Single Pregnant Women, and Gender Equity Programs for the 1996-97 and 1997-98 fiscal
years using a RFP or other competitive process. We noted that the most recent award of funds
for these programs using the RFP process was for the two-year grant allocation for the

1994-95 and 1995-96 fiscal years.

In response to our inquiry, FDOE management stated that a competitive process was not used
for the 1996-97 and 1997-98 allocations because, at the time appropriate to prepare and submit
RFPs, the Federal Government had not resolved issues regarding the continued funding of the
program. Under these circumstances, FDOE management considered it imprudent to distribute
RFPs as to have done so could have resulted in (1) proposals written for a defunct program,
(2) undue hardship for subgrantees notified at the last moment that program funds would not be
available to meet contracted salaries, and (3) funding cuts which would disqualify projects
from meeting program requirements that projects be of sufficient size, scope, and quality in
order to ensure effectiveness. Accordingly, the FDOE allocated the 1996-97 and 1997-98
funds based on the prior year (i.e., 1995-96 fiscal year) funding levels after taking into

consideration subgrantee performance in that prior year. Notwithstanding these circumstances,

Rehabilitation Services - Vocational
Rehabilitation Grants to States (CFDA
No. 84.126; State Agency, FDLES)

The Rehabilitation Services - Vocational Rehabilitation Grants to States (Rehabilitation
Services) Program was administered by two FDLES divisions: the Division of Vocational
Rehabilitation Services (DVR) and the Division of Blind Services (DBS). Our testing included

program administration and activities of both the DVR and the DBS.

Activities Allowed and Unallowed. Title 34, Section 361.24, Code of Federal Regulations,

requires the administering State agency to adopt policies and methods pertinent to the fiscal
administration and control of the Rehabilitation Services Program. Both the DVR and the DBS
have developed policies and procedures manuals which establish basic program requirements in
accordance with Federal and State laws and regulations. The DVR Operational Policies and
Procedures for VR Counselors manual (LESM 170-2), for example, requires that written
authorization by DVR staff be made either before or at the time services or commodities are
requested or purchased. The manual provides exceptions to this policy for emergency
situations and purchases of corollary services. Our review of 28 expenditures for client
services (25 DVR and 3 DBS), totaling $5,128, disclosed that invoices for 6 of the DVR
sampled expenditures, totaling $365, were authorized from 2 to 66 calendar days after the
services were either initiated or completed. (We also noted additional expenditures of $401
relating to those 6 sampled invoices that were authorized from 2 to 66 days after the services
were either initiated or completed.) The circumstances of these 6 instances did not justify
approval subsequent to the initiation or completion of the related services. Similar

circumstances were reported in audit report No. 12983, paragraph 285.

Allowable Costs/Cost Principles. OMB Circular A-87, Attachment B, Section 11.h., provides

that where employees are expected to work solely on a single Federal award or cost objective,
charges for their salaries and wages will be supported by periodic certifications that the
employees worked solely on that award/objective for the period covered by the certification.
These certifications are to be prepared at least semiannually and are to be signed by the
employee or supervisory official having firsthand knowledge of the work performed by the
employee. We reviewed salaries and other payroll-related expenditures (i.e., totaling $12,974)
associated with 19 FDLES employees (15 DVR and 4 DBS) which were charged solely to the
Rehabilitation Services Program during the 1996-97 State fiscal year. Our discussions with
applicable FDLES staff indicated that each of these employees worked solely on the
Rehabilitation Services Program during the period under audit. However, contrary to the
noted regulation, certifications were not obtained by the FDLES from these employees and/or
their supervisors indicating that the employees worked solely on that program during that
period. A similar finding was noted in audit report No. 12983, paragraph 288. Salary costs
charged directly to the Rehabilitation Services Program during the 1996-97 State fiscal year

totaled approximately $28 million.

Correspondence from FDLES personnel at both the DVR and the DBS indicated that
procedures correcting this situation are either currently in place or would be implemented in
the future. Specifically, in a memorandum dated February 13, 1998, addressed to our staff,
DVR personnel indicated that, effective December 1, 1997, all DVR employees’ monthly
“Individual Attendance and Leave Reports” (form HRM-53), which are signed by both the

employee and the employee’s supervisor, now require the employee to identify the grant

Eligibility - Required Reviews. Federal regulations require periodic reviews of the decisions

and determinations regarding the various vocational rehabilitation services provided program
clients and applicants. These reviews serve to ensure that the current status of applicable
individuals and related services are still in the best interest of the applicant/client and in
accordance with program regulations. As noted in the following paragraphs, our review
disclosed instances where the required reviews were not always timely performed and/or not

adequately documented.

Title 29, Section 706(22)(C)(iii)(I), United States Code, indicates that the provision of
vocational rehabilitation services for the purpose of making determinations regarding not only
whether an individual is eligible but, also, the nature and scope of the services needed (i.e.,
extended evaluation services), may be provided to an individual for a total period not longer
than 18 months. In addition, Title 29, Section 706(22)(C)(iii)(I]), United States Code, requires
that an assessment of the individual’s progress be documented at least once every 90 days
during which extended evaluation services are being provided. Our review of 25 case records

(19 DVR and 6 DBS) requiring extended evaluation services disclosed the following:

e The required 90-day reviews for 5 of the DVR and 2 of the DBS cases were not always
completed or were not always timely. Specifically, for 4 of the 5 DVR cases, instances
were noted in which reviews were performed 1 to 90 days late. No required reviews
were held for the remaining (fifth) case. Similarly, for the 2 DBS cases, instances were

noted in which reviews were performed 6 to 121 days late.

Eligibility - Required Case Actions/Documentation. Federal regulations stipulate that an

individual is eligible for vocational rehabilitation services if the individual has a physical or
mental impairment which for that individual constitutes or results in a substantial impediment
to employment and the individual requires such services to prepare for, enter, engage in, or
retain gainful employment. Generally, determinations of eligibility must be made within 60
days of the date that an individual submits an application for services. Moreover, as soon as a
determination has been made that an individual is eligible for such services, the designated
State unit should ensure, among other things, that an assessment for determining eligibility and
vocational rehabilitation needs is completed. Such documentation includes an individualized
written rehabilitation program (IWRP) that is to be jointly developed, agreed upon, and signed
by the individual (or authorized representative) and the vocational rehabilitation counselor or
coordinator. The IWRP is to be amended as needed to reflect the current status of planned

program services. Federal regulations also provide that, whenever the designated State unit

service costs. Section I costs are allocated to the various State agencies. The applicable State
agencies combine those allocated costs with other indirect costs incurred by the agencies in the
preparation of the agency indirect cost allocation plans. Indirect cost rates developed pursuant
to those agency plans are used by the agencies to charge indirect costs to Federal programs.

Section II costs are charged/billed to user State agencies/programs. To the extent that a user is

funded by a Federal program(s), the costs may be charged to those Federal programs.

For Section II of the 1998 FLSWCAP, the FEOG submitted information related to 13 billed
central services, i.e., central services that are billed to benefited agencies and/or programs on
an individual fee-for-service or similar basis. Ten of the 13 central services were accounted
for in internal service (or similar) funds. Two of the other 3 services were accounted for in
self insurance funds. The remaining central service represented employee fringe benefits (i.e.,
retirement benefits). Included with the information for the 10 internal service (or similar)
funds were reconciliation worksheets (i.e., Reconciliation of Fund to Federal Guidelines
Worksheets). The reconciliation worksheets were required to correctly identify the funds’
operating balances and any excessive billings that may have been charged to applicable Federal

programs.

The 1998 FLSWCAP was submitted to the USDHHS for review and approval on March 26,
1997. As a part of our audit of the 1998 FLSWCAP, we reviewed central services cost
support, allocation bases, reconciliations of the internal service funds, and other relevant
information. Although procedures were in place to properly allocate central service costs to
user departments (Section I costs) and to prepare accurate reconciliations of the internal service
funds and submit required information for billed services (Section II costs), our review

disclosed the following in regard to Section II costs:

e Clerical errors and inaccurate financial data were noted in 9 of the 10 internal service

(and similar) funds for which the Reconciliation of Fund to Federal Guidelines

Worksheets were tested. Specifically, our review of the reconciliation worksheets
disclosed that the beginning fund balances were incorrectly entered for 4 of the funds.
Federal guidelines provide that the balance to be brought forward from the prior year’s
reconciliation should be the lesser of the prior year's actual fund balance or the 60-day
expenditure equivalency amount. However, we noted that the balance brought forward
was something other than either of those two amounts. The errors resulted in differences

in the beginning fund balance ranging from an understatement of $256,000 to an

overstatement of $1,242,000.

Additionally, in the determination of total allowable expenditures, our review disclosed
that unallowable costs were improperly added to the expenditure totals for 6 of the funds
(including 2 of the funds noted above). These unallowable costs should have been
subtracted in calculating the total allowable expenditure amounts for those 6 funds.
These errors resulted in overstatements of allowable expenditures ranging from $73,000

to $722,000.

For the remaining internal service fund in which errors and inaccurate data were noted,
our review disclosed that the financial information included in the reconciliation
worksheet was compiled from the wrong trust fund. FEOG personnel indicated that the
applicable state agency, from whose financial records the information was obtained, was
provided a copy of the reconciliation worksheet for its review of the accuracy and
completeness of the data. The FEOG indicated that since no response was received from
the agency, it assumed that the data was accurate. In correspondence dated October 1,

1997, the FEOG notified the USDHHS that it was attempting to resolve the issue.

As similarly noted in audit report No. 12983, paragraph 322, and contrary to the
requirements of OMB Circular A-87, the 1998 FLSWCAP, as submitted, did not contain

all required information (i.e., actuarial reports or descriptions/explanations of

insurance funds and employee fringe benefits should be properly included, or documented

prior approval to not submit such information should be obtained from the USDHHS.

General (State Agency, FDCFS/FDOH)

Allowable Costs/Cost Principles. One of several cost allocation plans implemented by the

FDCFS is the Children and Families Services Cost Allocation Plan (CAP). This CAP

allocates costs relating to the provision of certain services, such as foster care and adoption
assistance, to appropriate funding sources based on a random moment sampling (RMS)
process. Under the RMS process, a sample of employees providing a range of services to
specific client groups is selected and the selected employees are then asked what specific
service and activity they are performing at a specific point in time within each quarter. The

responses from all sampled employees are accumulated in a manner to determine a cumulative

3 - 109
