employee effort on each specific service and activity. For example, the cumulative response
may indicate that 25 percent of all employee effort is for one service/activity that is funded by
one Federal program and the remaining 75 percent is on other services/activities that are

funded by various other sources. The percentages determined in this process are then used to

allocate the applicable costs incurred during that quarter to the appropriate funding sources.

As part of our audit we reviewed the FDCFS allocation of costs under this CAP for the quarter
ended June 30, 1997. Our tests included, among other things, a review of the FDCFS
accumulation of employee responses to the RMS process for that quarter and a review of 25
individual employee responses of the 2,300 employees who were sampled. Our test disclosed

the following deficiencies and resulting questioned costs:

e Due to an apparent software problem, the FDCFS made significant errors in the
classification of responses during the response accumulation process. For example,
responses that should have been classified as pertaining only to one service/activity were
improperly classified as pertaining to two separate services/activities. Based on our
analysis, the result was that the Child Welfare Services (CWS) - State Grants Program
(CFDA No. 93.645) was overcharged by $809,892, the Foster Care - Title IV-E (Title
IV-E) Program (CFDA No. 93.658) was undercharged by $805,641, and the Medical
Assistance Program (CFDA No. 93.778) was undercharged by $4,251.

e The purpose of the RMS is to allocate costs of services/activities benefiting more than
one program to appropriate funding sources based on the time and efforts of the
employees providing those services/performing those activities. Accordingly, only those
employee positions for which the related services/activities are to be funded from more
than one source should be included in the population of employee positions used in the
RMS process. For 1 of the 25 sampled responses selected for testing, we noted that the

applicable employee’s salary costs were charged as a direct cost to the Title IV-E

Cash Management. In order to implement the Cash Management Improvement Act (CMIA),

the State of Florida and the Secretary of the United States Department of the Treasury entered
into an agreement that was applicable to certain funds received from the Federal Government
during the 1996-97 State fiscal year. Section 6 of the CMIA Agreement requires State
agencies to request (draw) Federal funds so that they are received no earlier than the day the
corresponding warrant request vouchers are submitted to the State Comptroller for payment.
Clearance patterns, established to calculate the State’s interest liability, are based on the
premise that draws will be made in this manner. As similarly noted in prior audit reports,
most recently in audit report No. 12983, paragraph 338, our review disclosed that the FDCFS
(which also requested/received Federal funds on behalf of the FDOH during the State fiscal
year ended June 30, 1997) did not document that Federal funds were drawn, for those
programs covered by the CMIA Agreement, no earlier than the day the corresponding warrant
request vouchers were submitted to the State Comptroller. Specifically, during the 1996-97
fiscal year, decisions by the FDCFS to draw Federal funds were based on a daily analysis of
estimated cash position for each applicable trust fund. This analysis involved the comparison
of trust fund cash balances with the total of vouchers and other items (e.g., pending transfers)

that affect the cash balances. Generally, the trust funds at FDCFS accounted for the activities

are critical to an entity’s (auditee’s) subrecipient monitoring and oversight process. For
example, the determinations of the amount, timing, and degree of monitoring efforts should, in
part, be based on such summary records. Such summary records become more significant
under the revised Single Audit Act as fewer subrecipients will be subject to compliance audits
by independent auditors (i.e., the entity’s own monitoring efforts will be the primary
mechanism for ensuring subrecipient compliance with applicable laws, regulations, and
contractual terms and conditions). To ensure proper accountability and oversight of Federal
funds, we recommend that the FDCFS and the FDOH establish appropriate accounting records
that summarize, by Federal program, the amount of Federal funds provided to subrecipients.
(The major programs impacted by this finding include the Special Supplemental Nutrition

Subrecipient Monitoring - Audit Report Reviews. Pursuant to the provisions of the OMB

Circulars implementing the Single Audit Act (both the initial and revised acts), pass-through
entities (e.g., the FDCFS and the FDOH) must ensure that applicable subrecipients meet the
audit requirements of the Single Audit Act and submit the resulting audit reports to the
pass-through entities. The pass-through entities are to use such reports in determining whether
the subrecipients expended Federal funds, provided by the pass-through entities, in accordance
with governing laws, regulations, and applicable provisions of grant and contractual
agreements. In accordance with these requirements, the FDCFS established procedures
providing that the FDCFS Office of Internal Audit obtain and review audit reports for those
subrecipients receiving Federal funds from the FDCFS (and the FDOH through June 30,
1997). Those procedures provided for the documented review of the audit reports and follow
up on compliance issues, questioned costs, and/or internal control deficiencies relating to
FDCFS/FDOH-funded programs, as well as any audit report deficiencies (e.g., audit reports
did not meet applicable reporting standards such as omission of required reports or schedules).
Our test of 60 subrecipients required to meet the audit requirements of the Single Audit Act
and submit the resulting audit reports to the FDCFS during the 1996-97 fiscal year disclosed

that such reports were not always reviewed by the FDCFS in a timely manner. Specifically:

e For 6 audit reports the reviews by the FDCFS had not been completed as of the date of
our audit field work; although, periods ranging from 6 to 13 months had elapsed since
the FDCFS receipt of the audit reports. Findings in these 6 reports included questioned

costs and internal control deficiencies.

e For another 5 reports, FDCFS reviews had been completed as of the date of our audit
field work. However, the FDCFS reviews and/or FDCFS letters addressed to the
subrecipients and/or subrecipients’ auditors requesting appropriate corrective actions
were not timely. Specifically, in these 5 instances the respective reports were not

reviewed by the FDCFS for periods ranging from 6 months to 12 months after the

Childhood Immunization Grants (CFDA
No. 93.268; State Agency, FDOH)

Activities Allowed or Unallowed/Allowable Costs. Title 42, Section 51b.206, Code of Federal

Regulations, allows grant funds to be used to purchase supplies, materials, and equipment for
childhood immunization programs. Grant funds may also be used to pay for salaries or wages
and related expenses for personnel directly involved in the planning, organization, promotion,
epidemiology, surveillance, and other program activities. OMB Circular A-87 provides
additional guidance regarding Federal compensation for personnel services. The Circular
requires that when employees work on multiple activities or cost objectives, a distribution of
their salaries or wages should be supported by personnel activity reports or equivalent
documentation that meets specified standards. One of those standards requires that the
applicable employees sign personnel activity reports. For those employees who work on a
single Federal program or cost objective, the Circular requires that the related salary charges
should be supported by periodic certifications that the employee worked solely on that
program/cost objective for the period covered by the certification. Such certifications are to be
signed by the employee or a supervisory official having first hand knowledge of the work

performed by the applicable employee.

Of the $33,060,383 charged to the Childhood Immunization Program during the audit period,
$21,522,410 represented vaccine distributions, $1,417,304 represented salary charges, and the
remaining $10,120,669 represented other costs. As part of our audit, we selected a sample of
60 charges to the program. These 60 items were comprised of 30 vaccine distributions, 22
salary charges (sample population of $17,993), and 8 other administrative (e.g. travel,
supplies, equipment) charges (sample population of $4,312). Our tests of these items disclosed
the following reportable conditions and/or instances in which salaries and other administrative

costs, totaling $24,165, were not properly charged to the program. (Federal Grant Nos.
H23/CCH404429-07-7 and H23/CCH404429-08)

e As similarly noted in the audit report No. 12983, paragraphs 364 through 367, our tests
disclosed three FDOH County Health Department (CHD) employees (one each at
Putnam, Highlands, and Okeechobee CHDs) who indicated that their job duties included,
but were not limited to, immunization activities. However, each of the employees’
salaries was charged entirely to the Childhood Immunization Grants (CI) Program during
the sampled pay periods. In response to our inquiries, the applicable CHDs estimated
the time worked on immunization activities by the three employees was 23 percent, 60
percent, and 20 percent, respectively. Based on these estimates and FDOH recorded

salary charges, the CI Program was overcharged $23,565 during the audit period.

In response to our inquiries, staff of the applicable CHDs indicated, in part, that prior to
the 1997-98 State fiscal year there was little emphasis on charging CHD personnel costs
to particular accounting codes. Furthermore, the CHDs did not have the level of systems
necessary to effectively manage data (e.g., salary costs) charged to specific accounting
codes. For example, when one employee worked on several programs and cost
objectives, it was difficult and time-consuming to adjust the charges that may have been
coded to a single accounting code representing one program/cost objective. The CHDs

also responded, in part, that the capability to effectively manage these salary charges has

now been developed, and that capability should ensure salary costs are appropriately

During the audit period, CHD employees were required to complete employee activity
records that accounted for the time spent each workday on each of the various programs
administered by the CHDs, including the CI Program. However, contrary to OMB
Circular A-87, these employee activity records were not always signed by the employees
or supervisory officials knowledgeable of the employees’ activities. Furthermore, during
the audit period these employee activity records were not retained for a period exceeding
six months. Effective July 1, 1997 (subsequent to our audit period), the FDOH initiated
a policy providing for periodic signed certifications for those CHD employees working
on one or more program or cost objective. Additionally, the FDOH indicated that it
would revise its policy, effective October 1, 1997, to retain the employee activity records

for postaudit purposes.

During our review of the St. Johns CHD, we noted that a specialized health and safety
record was purchased and provided to parents for the purpose of providing them
information on child health and safety, and as a means of documenting medical histories
and other descriptive information (e.g., childhood immunizations) specific to their
children. The cost of the specialized record was $600, which was charged entirely to the
CI Program. In response to our inquiry, the FDOH Program Office stated that the
acquisition was not an allowable use of CI Program funds. Accordingly, the $600 cost
of the records should have been charged to another funding source(s). The Program

Office indicated that the CI Program would be reimbursed for this acquisition.

To ensure that CI Program funds are used only for authorized and appropriate purposes, we
recommend that the FDOH continue efforts to establish methodologies that will provide for the
equitable allocation of salary costs and the maintenance of supporting documentation in

accordance with controlling Federal requirements. Additionally, the FDOH should ensure that

only allowable costs are charged to the CI Program.

Maintenance of Effort. Childhood Immunization Program funds may be used to supplement,

but not supplant, existing immunization services and operations provided by the State. To
demonstrate compliance with this maintenance of effort requirement, it is incumbent upon
management to establish a system of controls that will enable them to determine all program
expenditures funded from non-Federal sources. As similarly noted in audit report No. 12983,
paragraphs 369 through 371, the FDOH did not have a methodology in place during the audit
period to identify expenditures paid from non-Federal sources and, therefore, demonstrate that
the State had met the maintenance of effort requirement. Subsequent to the audit period, the
FDOH developed a methodology for identifying non-Federal program expenditures and
submitted a request for approval of the methodology to the Federal Center for Disease Control
and Prevention on September 19, 1997. We recommend that the FDOH continue to pursue a
Federally-approved methodology for measuring non-Federal program expenditures. Once such
a methodology is established, the FDOH should monitor expenditures to ensure that

State-funding levels are maintained.

Opinion Qualification. Because of the lack of methodology to demonstrate compliance with

maintenance of effort requirements as noted in paragraph 158, the FDOH system of internal
control was not operating in a manner to ensure that the CI Program was administered

materially in compliance with program regulations.

Temporary Assistance for Needy Families

and Family Support Payments to States -
Assistance Payments Programs Cluster (CFDA
Nos. 93.558 and 93.560; State Agency, FDCFS)

General. The Federal Personal Responsibility and Work Opportunity Reconciliation Act of
1996 (Public Law 104-193) eliminated the open-ended Federal entitlement program of Family
Support Payments to States - Assistance Payments (known as AFDC) and created a block grant
for States to provide time-limited assistance for needy families. The block grant is entitled
Temporary Assistance for Needy Families (TANF). To participate in the TANF Program the
State had to submit a State Plan to the Federal Government. The State’s Work and Gain
Economic Self-Sufficiency (WAGES) Act (Chapter 96-175, Laws of Florida) provides the
blueprint for the State’s Plan. The TANF Program was implemented effective October 1,
1996. Because the TANF Program was not implemented until after the first of the audit period
(i.e., July 1, 1996), many compliance requirements (e.g., maintenance of effort, reporting

requirements, minimum participation, etc.) cannot be tested until subsequent audits.

Activities Allowed or Unallowed/Cost Principles. OMB Circular A-87, Attachment B, Section

11.h., provides that salaries of employees who work on a single Federal award or cost
objective should be supported by periodic certifications that the employees worked solely on
that program/cost objective for the period covered by the certification. This Section also
provides that salaries and wages of employees chargeable to more than one Federal award or
other cost objective should be supported by personnel activity reports or equivalent
documentation. That documentation is to be used as the basis to equitably allocate those
salaries and wages to the benefiting programs and/or cost objectives. A substitute system may
be used to allocate salary costs to benefiting programs/objectives (i.e., in lieu of personnel
activity reports) if approved by the cognizant Federal agency. Salaries and wages of
employees used in meeting cost-sharing or matching requirements of Federal awards must be

supported in the same manner as those claimed as allowable costs under Federal awards.

Our test of 80 program charges (60 TANF/20 AFDC) included 18 administrative expenditures,
of which 11 represented FDCFS salary charges. Our testing of those salary charges disclosed
that, contrary to the requirements of OMB Circular A-87, periodic certifications were not
obtained by the FDCFS for one employee who worked solely on TANF Program activities and
whose salary was funded 60 percent from TANF funds and 40 percent from State funds (i.e.,
for TANF maintenance of effort requirements). For another four employees to which our
sampled charges pertained, the FDCFS did not maintain personnel activity reports or
equivalent documentation to support the allocation of the employees’ salary costs to benefiting

programs, including the TANF Program. There was no Federally-approved substitute system

for allocating a portion of these employees’ salary costs to the TANF Program.

The salary costs charged to the TANF Program for these five employees during the 1996-97
fiscal year totaled $77,880. (Federal Grant No. GO9701FLTANF) Total salary costs charged
to the TANF Program during that period were $32,879,957. In response to our audit
inquiries, the FDCFS indicated that it was aware that changes were needed to the existing
FDCFS system for charging costs to the TANF Program (e.g., through amendments to the
existing FDCFS cost allocation plan as a “Federally-approved substitute system for charging
costs to various programs/cost objectives”). The FDCFS indicated that it was reluctant to
initiate and request approval for any such modifications until finalization of Federal regulations
for the TANF Program. Notwithstanding the FDCFS reason, we recommend that the FDCFS
maintain the records required by OMB Circular A-87 and/or obtain approval for a substitute
system for the allocation of salary costs to the TANF Program. Until such corrective measures
are taken, the FDCFS cannot demonstrate that salary costs are being equitably allocated to the

TANF Program.

e The TANF State Plan requires that the benefit payment amount be determined by
subtracting, from the applicable payment standard, the monthly net income calculated in
accordance with the plan. The State Plan specifies that the payment standard used in the

sampled payments, we noted that an incorrect payment standard was used in the benefit

the 1996-97 fiscal year. As one of these cases received AFDC benefits prior to the
TANF Program, and benefit levels for the AFDC Program were calculated similar to
that for the TANF Program, these errors also resulted in overpayments of AFDC
benefits, totaling $423, during the 1996-97 fiscal year.

e The FDCFS was not, as of March 9, 1998, able to provide a complete case file for one
household represented by the 60 sampled payments. Without this complete case file, the
FDCES has not documented that benefits paid to the household were proper. Payments
to the household during the 1996-97 fiscal year totaled $1,524 and $606 for the TANF
and AFDC Programs, respectively. (Federal Grant Nos. G9701FLTANF and
G9604FL4007)

In summary, in 5 of 60 benefit payments/case files tested, exceptions relative to benefits,
eligibility, and/or program requirements were noted. We recommend that appropriate

Eligibility (AFDC only). In accordance with Title 45, Section 205.40, Code of Federal

Regulations, the FDCFS established quality control units that reviewed the accuracy and
propriety of FDCFS eligibility determinations and benefit payments relative to the AFDC
Program. These reviews were used to develop the State’s quality control payment error rates.
Erroneous payments were those payments to ineligible assistance units and overpayments of
benefits to eligible assistance units. An assistance unit (i.e., household) included all
individuals whose needs, income, and resources were considered in determining eligibility for,
and the amount of, a benefit payment. Title 45, Section 205.43, Code of Federal Regulations,
set forth the rules and procedures for calculating State AFDC error rates and for disallowing
Federal financial participation to States with erroneous payments in excess of the national
standard for a fiscal year. Specifically, if a State’s overpayment error rate for a fiscal year
exceeded the national standard, Federal matching funds to the State were to be reduced. The
national standard represents the national overpayment rate for the fiscal year or 4 percent,

whichever was larger.

As noted in recent audit reports, most recently in report No. 12983, paragraph 374, the
USDHHS imposed a fiscal sanction (disallowance): of $12,085,205 on the State as the FDCFS
overpayment error rate of 9.66 percent for the 1990-91 Federal fiscal year (FFY) exceeded the
national standard of 4.96 percent established for that year. As reported, the FDCFS appealed
the disallowance on August 29, 1994. We reported that the appeal had not been decided and
that the FDCES indicated that the initial sanction has been reduced to $11,630,101 as the result
of the FDCFS being credited for AFDC overpayments collected through FDCFS Benefit
Recovery Units (BRUs). As of December 22, 1997, the appeal still had not been decided. In
correspondence to us dated January 8, 1998, FDCES staff indicated the case is expected to go

to trial in Federal court on March 28, 1998.

(AFDC/TANF). Foster Care (FC) cases are established based on referrals from the FDCFS
Title IV-E Program. For those PA and FC cases, collections of child support from absent
parents are used to reimburse the State for the cash assistance benefits paid to the custodial
parents and/or other parties on behalf of the applicable children. Nonpublic assistance (NA)
cases are established for those individuals applying for services pursuant to Title 45, Section
302.33, Code of Federal Regulations (i.e., individuals not receiving AFDC/TANF or Foster
Care assistance). The last basic type represents cases established based on referrals from the
FDCFS for individuals participating as clients in the Medicaid Program, but not also
participating in the AFDC/TANF Program (Medicaid-only cases). For purposes of our

testing, these Medicaid-only cases were included in the NA case category because of the

similar requirements applicable to both case types.

Pursuant to Title 45, Section 303.2, Code of Federal Regulations, the FDOR is required to
establish a case record and to determine the necessary actions to be taken (i.e., solicit
necessary information from the custodial parent and other relevant sources, initiate verification
of information, etc.) within no more than 20 calendar days of receipt of the referral of the case
or receipt of the application for CSE services. The case record must include all information
and documents pertaining to the case, as well as all relevant facts, dates, actions taken,

contacts made, and results.

The FDOR uses both the FDCFS Florida On-line Recipient Integrated Data Access
(FLORIDA) System and paper files to record and maintain the required information for CSE
cases. A CSE case is considered established when an interactive interview has been
completed. An interactive interview represents the completion of a series of interview screens
on the FLORIDA System based on information solicited from the custodial parent or from
information otherwise referred/obtained with the case. For FC cases in which both parents are

absent parents, the interview consists of obtaining information on each of the absent parents

Our examination of the FDOR's compliance with case establishment requirements included a
review of a sample of 64 cases that should have been established during the 1996-97 State
fiscal year. Our review disclosed that for 20 of the 40 PA items tested (50 percent) and 4 of
the 8 FC items tested (50 percent), the FDOR had not established cases prior to our request on
February 13, 1998, or approximately seven months after the end of the fiscal year in which the
cases should have been established. We also noted, for the 20 PA cases and 4 FC cases in
which an interactive interview was conducted, that in 19 instances (15 PA and 4 FC) the
interview was not held within 20 days. The delays in conducting these interviews ranged from
6 days to approximately 13 months after the end of the respective 20-day periods. In 4 of the
15 PA cases for which the interviews were not timely, FDOR records attribute the delays, in
part, to the custodial parents’ failure to attend appointments that had been scheduled by FDOR
staff within the required 20-day period. Notwithstanding those 4 instances, the delays in the

establishment of the CSE cases were generally attributable by the FDOR to the following:

e Difficulties with the interface of the PA and CSE components within the FLORIDA

System, which resulted in a backlog of PA cases awaiting initiation by the CSE Program.

e Difficulties in obtaining information related to FC cases, which contain confidential

information that is often sealed pursuant to a court order.

Delays in the establishment of CSE cases may cause delays in the collection of funds
reimbursable, in part, to the Federal Government. Similar deficiencies were noted in prior
audit reports, most recently in audit report No. 12983, paragraphs 393 through 396. In its
Summary Schedule of Prior Audit Findings dated November 19, 1997, the FDOR indicated

that, in regard to these deficiencies, contracts had been awarded to two private entities for the

Special Tests and Provisions - Location Efforts. Title 45, Section 303.3(b)(3), Code of

Federal Regulations, requires the FDOR to access all appropriate location sources within 75
days of determining that location of absent parents is necessary. The investigative process to
locate an absent parent begins upon the FDOR's establishment of a case, as described in
paragraph 178, based on the receipt of absent parent information from the FDCFS or directly
from the custodial parent. Local "locate" sources may include, but are not limited to, the
custodial parent, directories, the United States Postal Service, employers, and correctional
institutions. The FDOR also has a Central Parent Locator (CPL) unit that may be used as a
locating source. Upon request from the regions, the CPL unit will search other State "locate"
sources. Within the FLORIDA System, many of those location efforts are automated. For
example, upon the establishment of a new case, requests may automatically be sent to various
State and/or other entities through the System's data exchange module. Additionally, the
FDOR has access to the Federal Parent Locator Service (FPLS). During the audit period,

requests to the FPLS were manually initiated by FDOR staff.

Of the 37 (18 PA, 15 NA, and 4 FC) cases in which the FDOR had enough information to
pursue location of the absent parents, we were not provided documentation for 9 cases (6 PA,
1 NA, and 2 FC) evidencing that all appropriate “locate” sources were utilized by the FDOR
within the required 75-day period. For 8 (5 PA, 1 NA, and 2 FC) of these 9 cases, the FPLS
was not used although the absent parents were not located or were not located for periods

ranging from 91 to 451 days after the dates that the respective interactive interviews were held.

Special Tests and Provisions - Case Type Classifications. CSE cases are designated by

“type.” Within each of the 4 basic types noted in paragraph 176 are subclassifications (or
types). For example, a PA case may be designated as a case in which the custodial parent is
currently receiving public assistance or a case in which the custodial parent formerly received
public assistance. The proper recording of case types is essential, as the distributions of
collections are determined, in part, on the case type. Additionally, the implementation of any
sanctions (i.e., reducing custodial parent’s public assistance) as a result of noncooperation by a
custodial parent may be delayed, or not done, if the case type is not properly
assigned/recorded. During our tests of the 40 PA referrals and related cases we noted 8
instances in which the case types recorded in the FLORIDA System were incorrect. For
example, cases were coded to indicate that the custodial parent was currently receiving PA
assistance when that parent was no longer receiving those benefits. Furthermore, our tests of
16 NA cases disclosed that, for 1 instance in which the custodial parent subsequently received
public assistance, the case type was not timely revised from a NA case to a PA case. These
findings were attributed by FDOR staff to deficiencies within the FLORIDA System. Similar

findings were noted in audit report No. 12983, paragraph 402. To ensure the proper

OCSE-156 Report discloses data relating to case inventory, services required, services
provided, and interstate activity. The OCSE-158 Report discloses statistical data relating to

receivable, staff, and program expenditures. The OCSE-34 Report discloses data on the
amount of collections received, the corresponding distributions, and the amount of

undistributed collections for applicable categories.

acknowledged that the reported balance of undistributed child support enforcement collections
(i.e., $36,385,987 as of June 30, 1997) continued to be based on data from a previous system

Reporting - Periodic Reconciliations. Except as noted in paragraph 186, our test of the CSE

Quarterly Report of Collections for the quarter ended March 31, 1997, disclosed that the

However, we noted that, for the period July 1, 1996, through June 30, 1997, the FDOR had

FLORIDA System to collections recorded in the State Automated Management Accounting
Subsystem (SAMAS), which is the official accounting system for the State of Florida.

deficiencies were noted in audit report No. 12983, paragraph 412. In response to our current
inquiry, FDOR management indicated that FDOR program staff were in the process of writing
procedures for reconciling all financial transactions that flow through the FLORIDA System
and the SAMAS records and that completion of those procedures was anticipated by April 30,
1998. Without timely and periodic reconciliations, the FDOR does not have in place the
controls necessary to provide assurance as to the validity of the FLORIDA System data. We
recommend that the FDOR continue its efforts to implement procedures that ensure periodic

reconciliations are performed in a timely manner.

Cash Management. Section 6.3.25 of the 1996-97 fiscal year Cash Management Improvement

Act (CMIA) Agreement between United States Department of the Treasury and the State of
Florida required that the preissuance funding technique be applied for the direct program costs
of the CSE Program administered by the FDOR. The preissuance funding technique is a
method of transferring Federal funds to a state prior to the date the state issues warrants or
initiates electronic fund transfer payments. Under this technique, the state incurs an interest
liability to the Federal Government from the day Federal funds are credited to a state account

to the day the state pays out the funds for purposes of the applicable Federal programs.

For child support collections received by the State on behalf of recipients of Title IV-A
assistance programs (i.e., AFDC/TANF Program cash benefits), the State is required to retain
a share of those collections as reimbursement for the benefits paid pursuant to those programs.
A portion of those retained collections must be credited to the Federal Government. That
portion represents the Federal share of the Title IV-A benefits paid to the applicable recipients.
Prior to October 1996, the Federal share of child support collections were required to be
transferred by the FDOR to the State Title IV-A agency (FDCFS) and credited by the FDCFS
to the applicable Title IV-A Program (i.e., AFDC Program). However, effective October
1996, Federal instructions provided that the Federal share of applicable child support
collections were to be retained by the State’s CSE Program and used to fund the CSE Program
operations. Accordingly, the amount of Federal CSE Program funds requested and received
directly from the Federal Government (i.e., pursuant to the preissuance funding technique)
were to be reduced by the amounts of the retained child support collections (i.e., Federal
share). To reduce the State’s interest liability to the Federal Government pursuant to the
CMIA, such retained child support collections should be applied to the next available request
of Federal CSE Program funds. Specifically, upon the determination of the amount of child
support funds retained, the next request for Federal funds should be reduced in the amount of

those retained collections (i.e., such that less Federal funds are requested). Requesting less

Federal funds reduces the amount to which the CMIA Agreement interest factor is applied and

thereby reduces the State’s interest liability.

In the initial implementation of this provision that was effective October 1996, we noted that
the FDOR did not timely apply the Federal share of retained child support collections to
subsequent requests (draws) of Federal CSE Program funds. Specifically, while the FDOR
determined the amounts of retained child support collections on January 31, 1997, in one
instance and April 30, 1997, in another instance, the FDOR did not apply those amounts to
subsequent draws (i.e., reduce amount drawn) until March 1997 and May 1997, respectively.
Had the FDOR timely applied those retained amounts to the next available draw, the State’s

interest liability would have been reduced by approximately $42,600.

To minimize the State’s interest liability, we recommend that the FDOR establish procedures
to ensure that appropriate and timely communications, regarding the amount of child support

collections retained, are made to the FDOR personnel responsible for drawing Federal funds.

administered by the FDCFS and (2) the Child Support Enforcement Program (CFDA 93.563)
administered by the FDOR. The results of our review will be disclosed in a separate report

issued by our Office.

Opinion Qualification. Because of the lack of timely establishment of case files and

initiation/completion of required case actions as noted in paragraphs 176 through 184, because
required and accurate data were not always reported to the Federal Government as noted in
paragraphs 185 through 187, and because of the lack of periodic reconciliations of collections
recorded in the SAMAS to collections recorded in the FDCFS FLORIDA System as noted in

Refugee and Entrant Assistance - State
Administered Programs (CFDA No.
93.566; State Agency, FDCFS)

Activities Allowed or Unallowed/Cost Principles/Eligibility. Title 45, Section 400.209, Code

of Federal Regulations, provides that Refugee and Entrant Assistance - State Administered
Programs (REAP) funds may not be used to fund expenditures for cash and medical assistance
provided to refugees within a filing unit (assistance group) who have been in the United States
longer than specified periods. Pursuant to Title 45, Sections 400.203(b) and 400.204(b), Code
of Federal Regulations, REAP funds can be used to fund benefits under the Refugee Cash
Assistance (RCA) and Refugee Medical Assistance (RMA) programs for the eight-month
period after the applicable refugee’s entry into the United States. The RCA and RMA
programs use REAP funds to pay 100 percent of the cash and medical assistance to eligible
filing units that do not qualify for AFDC/TANF and/or Medicaid. Our tests of 60 client files
disclosed 12 instances in which medical benefits, totaling $28,393, were provided for periods

ranging from 1 to 14 months beyond the time period allowed by Federal regulations.

Federal regulations governing the REAP, such as Title 45, Sections 400.28 and 400.43, Code
of Federal Regulations, require that determinations of eligibility and the individuals receiving
assistance be supported by appropriate documentation, such as recipient case records.

Examples of appropriate and required documentation include proper Federal Immunization and

aware, subsequent to the initial eligibility determinations, that the applicable clients
earned income was greater than the established standard (threshold). However, in these
instances the FDCFS did not take timely action to terminate benefits to those clients.
Based on FDCFS records and responses to our inquiries, periods from the date that
FDCFS became aware that the clients were no longer eligible to the date that benefits
terminated ranged from one to five months. Benefits improperly paid to and/or on behalf
of these applicable clients totaled $2,661. In 2 of these 6 instances, the FDCFS provided
documentation showing that the overpayments had been detected by FDCFS staff prior to
our inquiry and that the resulting overpayments had been referred to the appropriate
party for recovery of benefit overpayments. (One of these instances is also included in
the 12 instances noted above in which medical benefits were provided beyond the

allowed periods.)

We noted one instance in which the children of an assistance group were not properly
considered in determining the number of individuals in the assistance group and the
respective benefit amounts. In this instance, two children were inappropriately
considered as assistance groups separate from their parents. As a result, benefits were

overpaid in the amount of $486.

The above instances pertaining to payments beyond the allowed periods, eligibility not
adequately documented, and improper benefit payments resulted in questioned costs totaling
$56,431. (Federal Grant Nos. G-96-AA-FL-4100 and G-97-AA-FL-4100) Similar findings
were noted in numerous audit reports, most recently in audit report No. 12983, paragraphs 426

through 428. We again recommend that the FDCFS implement appropriate corrective actions to

certifications from applicable employees in accordance with OMB Circular A-87.

General. The Federal Personal Responsibility and Work Opportunity Reconciliation Act of
1996 (Public Law 104-193) combined the Child Care Development Block Grant (CCDBG)
Program with two other Federal funding sources to create the Child Care and Development
Fund (CCDF) Program. The CCDF Program was implemented effective October 1, 1996.
Because the CCDF Program was not implemented until after the first of our audit period (i.e.,
July 1, 1996), certain compliance requirements (e.g., matching, reporting requirements, etc.)

relating to this program cannot be tested until subsequent audits.

Activities Allowed and Unallowed. As part of our audit we selected and tested a sample of 61

charges to the CCDBG and the CCDF Programs. These sampled charges, totaling
$1,896,840, included, for example: (1) payments to central agencies (i.e., contracted
subrecipients of the FDCFS) as reimbursement for payments by the central agencies to entities
providing applicable child care services and (2) administrative expenditures such as salaries.

Our tests disclosed questioned costs, totaling $254,884, as described in the following:

e For two sample payments to one central agency, the FDCFS did not provide adequate
support for the disbursements. Although the central agency’s invoices were provided for
the payments, copies of the related attendance records evidencing that the related services
had been provided to eligible children were not provided for our review. These two

payments totaled $239,944.

e The employee to whom one sampled salary charge pertained did not work on the

salary costs noted above should be reimbursed to the program. (Federal Grant Nos.

G94B1FLCARE and G971FLCCDF)

Earmarking Requirements. Title 42, Sections 618(b)(2) and 9858, United States Code,

contain specific requirements concerning the use of CCDF funds. Specifically, not more than
5 percent of the aggregate amount of funds available to a State in a fiscal year may be
expended for administrative costs and not less than 70 percent of Mandatory and Matching
CCDF funds (i.e., categories of CCDF awards) must be used to provide child care assistance
to specific client groups. To ensure compliance with these earmarking requirements, the
FDCFS has established unique accounting codes to capture CCDF expenditures within the
applicable spending categories. For example, funds used to pay for child care services should

be recorded separately from those funds used for administrative costs.

Our tests of 61 expenditures, as noted above in paragraph 205, disclosed 1 instance in which a
payment to a central agency for administrative activities, totaling $8,075, was improperly
recorded in the FDCFS accounting records as expenditures for child care services. Similar
findings were noted in previous audit reports, most recently in audit report No. 12983,
paragraph 432. (Federal Grant No. G9701FLCCDF) Accurate recording of expenditures is

necessary for the FDCFS to demonstrate compliance with the noted earmarking requirements.

Reporting Requirements. Title 45, Sections 98.70 and 98.71(a), Code of Federal Regulations,

require the FDCFS to prepare and submit an annual report (ACF-700) covering the most
recent program period, specifying the uses for which CCDBG funds were expended and the
amount expended for such uses. Title 45, Section 98.64, Code of Federal Regulations,
requires the FDCFS to prepare and submit an annual Financial Status Report (SF-269) for each

fiscal year’s grant.

The SF-269 reports for the Federal fiscal year (FFY) ended September 30, 1996, reported total
expenditures for the 1994 FFY CCDBG grant award in the amount of $18,197,096 and total
expenditures for the 1995 FFY CCDBG grant award in the amount of $28,658,260. Our review
of the ACF-700 and SF-269 reports for the Federal fiscal year ended September 30, 1996,
disclosed that total expenditures reported on the ACF-700 for the 1994 grant award were
$156,800 less than the corresponding SF-269 report and total expenditures reported on the
ACF-700 for the 1995 grant award were $2,543,946 less than the SF-269 report submitted for
that award. The differences were attributed to an overstatement (i.e., $2,173,462) of the two
SF-269 reports due to the use of financial data as of December 12, 1996, rather than September
30, 1996; the omission of indirect costs on the ACF-700 report; and a clerical error on the ACF-
700 report. Subsequent to our inquiry, revised reports were prepared by the FDCFS. To assist
the Federal Government in their oversight responsibilities, we recommend that the FDCFS
improve efforts to properly report expenditure information on future reports. We also
recommend that the financial portion of these two reports be compared and/or reconciled prior to
submission of the reports to the Federal Government. (Federal Grant Nos. G94B1FLCARE and
G95B1IFLCARE)

(SA 97-211) Prior Audit Status - Special Tests and Provisions. Pursuant to Title 45, Section 1356.21(c),
Code of Federal Regulations, and as defined by Title 42, Section 675(5)(B), Unites States

Code, the FDCFS is required to establish a case review system for ensuring that the status of
each child placed in foster care is reviewed periodically but no less frequently than once every
six months. These reviews are to be conducted either by a court or by an administrative
review in order to determine the continuing necessity for and appropriateness of the placement,
the extent of compliance with the case plan, and the extent of progress toward alleviating or
mitigating the causes necessitating placement in foster care, and to project a likely date by
which the child may be returned to the home or placed for adoption or legal guardianship. As
similarly noted in several prior audit reports, most recently in report No. 12983, paragraph
492, our review of 30 client files disclosed 4 instances in which documentation was not
available to evidence that the required reviews had been conducted within the prescribed time

periods. Specifically:

e For 2 instances, documentation provided showed delays of 14 and 19 days beyond the

required dates.

e For 1 instance, documentation showed that no reviews were administered on behalf of
the child after September 25, 1996. A subsequent review was due no later than March

25, 1997. Therefore, as of December 30, 1997, the review was 280 days late.

e For 1 instance, documentation showed that no reviews were administered on behalf of
the client after November 28, 1995. A subsequent review was due no later than May 28,

1996. Therefore, as of December 23, 1997, the review was 574 days late.

(SA 97-212) Some of the delays may have been due to judicial concerns. In the Summary Schedule of Prior

Audit Findings, the FDCFS indicated that corrective actions were being implemented, with an

Activities Allowed or Unallowed/Cost Principles. OMB Circular A-87, Attachment B, Section

11.h., provides that salaries and wages of employees chargeable to more than one Federal
award or multiple activities or other cost objectives should be supported by personnel activity
reports or equivalent documentation, unless a statistical sampling system or other substitute
system has been approved by the cognizant Federal agency. The documentation method used
should reflect the distribution of the actual total activity for which each employee is
compensated. That documentation is to be used to equitably charge the applicable Federal
awards, activities, and cost objectives. The 60 FDCFS expenditures charged to the Foster
Care - Title IV-E (Title IV-E) Program and selected for review included 3 payroll items. As
part of our review, we interviewed the applicable employees and/or their supervisors. The
employees and/or their supervisors indicated that, in addition to the Title IV-E Program, the
employees’ duties and responsibilities related to other programs in the FDCFS Family Safety

and Preservation Program Office.

Two of the three applicable employees were administrative assistants responsible for assisting
FDCFS attorneys who provided legal representation for juvenile dependency actions. Of the
salary costs, totaling $49,569, incurred on behalf of these two employees during the 1996-97
fiscal year, the FDCFS allocated approximately $12,500 (Federal share was 50 percent or
$6,250) to the Title [V-E Program. This allocation was based on a pro rata share determined
by comparing hours worked on the Title IV-E Program to total hours worked by applicable
FDCFS attorneys and paralegals. Personnel activity records or equivalent documentation were

not maintained for these two employees. While the allocation method used may be reasonable,

documentation was not provided to show that this method was approved by the cognizant
Federal agency as a substitute system (i.e., such that individual personnel activity reports or

equivalent documentation meeting OMB Circular A-87 standards would not be required).

In regard to the third employee, the FDCFS incurred salary costs of $51,689. Of those costs,
approximately $4,700 were allocated and charged to the Title IV-E Program (Federal share
was $2,350). While no clear basis for the allocation of those costs to the Title [V-E Program
was provided in response to our inquiry, available FDCFS records indicate that the allocation
basis for those costs may have been employee or client “headcount.” Personnel activity
reports or equivalent documentation were not maintained for this employee that reflected the
hours worked/effort expended on the Title IV-E Program. While an allocation method based
on headcount may be reasonable, documentation was not provided to substantiate the actual
allocation basis for the noted costs, or to show that a substitute method had been approved by
the cognizant Federal agency (i.e., such that personnel activity reports or equivalent

documentation meeting OMB Circular A-87 standards would not be required).

Salary costs charged to the Title IV-E Program during the 1996-97 fiscal year and impacted by
the above-noted deficiencies totaled $1,277,623 (Federal share $638,813). (Federal Grant
Nos. 9601FL1401, 9701FL1401, 9601FL1404, and 9701FL1404) To ensure that the program
is equitably charged its share of salary costs, we recommend that the FDCFS maintain the
personnel activity records required by OMB Circular A-87 and allocate the applicable salary

costs based on those records, or obtain Federal approval for a substitute system.

for assistance under the Family Support Payments to States — Assistance Payments (AFDC)

We tested 60 client files established on behalf of children who received Title IV-E Program
funded maintenance payments during the audit period. Our review disclosed one instance in
which the case file documented that both parents were in the home at the time of removal (i.e.,
there was no deprivation of parental care). Title IV-E Program funded benefits paid on behalf
of this client totaled $2,599. This error was detected by the FDCFS subsequent to our request
for the applicable case file. The file records indicate that the error resulted from incorrect
coding of the funding source for the maintenance payments but that corrective action was to be
taken. We recommend that the Foster Care Title IV-E Program be credited for these
improperly funded payments and that actions be taken to ensure that Title IV-E Program
moneys are used to fund foster care maintenance payments only on behalf of eligible children.

(Federal Grant Nos. 9601FL1401 and 9701FL1401)

Eligibility - Case Plans. Title 42, Section 671(a)(16), United States Code, provides that a case

plan be developed for each child receiving foster care maintenance payments. Title 45,
Section 1356.21(d), Code of Federal Regulations, requires the case plan to be a written
document, which is a discrete part of the case record, and in a format determined by the State.
In addition, the case plan must be developed within a reasonable period to be established by the
State, but in no event later than 60 days starting at the time the State agency assumes
responsibility for providing services, including placing the child. The regulation also requires
the case plan to include a discussion of how the plan is designed to achieve a placement in the

least restrictive setting available and in close proximity to the home of the parent(s), consistent

Our test of 60 client files included 15 clients who received their initial Title [V-E Program
funded benefits in the 1996-97 fiscal year and 45 who received their initial Title [V-E Program

funded benefits prior to that fiscal year. Our tests of these client files disclosed the following:

e Case plans for 3 of the 15 clients who received their initial benefits during the 1996-97
fiscal year were not timely prepared. The delays in preparation for 2 of these cases were
101 and 136 days. In the remaining instance, the case plan was not signed or dated.
However, based on the court hearing date to approve the plan, this case plan was
prepared 40 days beyond the required date. Similar instennss were noted in audit report

No. 12983, paragraphs 450 and 451.

e No case file was available for 1 of the 45 clients who received their initial benefits prior
to the 1996-97 fiscal year. In this instance, the client was removed from her home and
taken into custody of the Florida Department of Health and Rehabilitative Services
(predecessor to the FDCFS) on April 22, 1993. Title IV-E Program funded benefits paid
on behalf of this client during the 1996-97 fiscal year totaled $4,411. (Federal Grant
Nos. 9601FL1401 and 9701FL1401)

To ensure compliance with controlling Federal regulations and assure that services appropriate
to the children’s needs are identified and goals related to the children’s placement are
established, we recommend that initial case plans be timely prepared and maintained. Timely
preparation of case plans will also promote the economic and efficient provision of services.
Additionally, we recommend that the FDCFS reimburse the Title [V-E Program for the one

instance in which no evidence was provided to demonstrate that a case plan was prepared.

Eligibility - Required Reviews. Pursuant to Title 45, Section 1356.21(c), Code of Federal

Regulations, and as defined by Title 42, Section 675(5)(B), United States Code, a case review
system is required to ensure that the status of each child placed in foster care is reviewed
periodically but no less frequently than once every six months. These reviews are to be
conducted either by a court or by administrative review in order to determine the continuing
necessity for and appropriateness of the placement, the extent of compliance with the case
plan, and the extent of progress made toward alleviating or mitigating the causes necessitating
placement in foster care, and to project a likely date by which the child may be returned to the

home or placed for adoption or legal guardianship.

As similarly noted in prior audit reports, most recently in report No. 12983, paragraphs 448
and 449, our review of 45 client files disclosed 16 instances in which documentation that the
required reviews had been conducted within the prescribed time periods was not available.
Documentation provided by the FDCFS for 15 of these 16 instances indicated that the required
reviews had been conducted from 8 to 1,292 days after the required dates. In 3 of these 15
instances, the reviews were less than 30 days late and in another 3 of the instances the reviews
were more than one year late. In the remaining instance, there was no evidence of any
required reviews during the 1996-97 fiscal year. Notwithstanding that other forms of
monitoring (e.g., FDCFS Social Study Report/Case Plan Update) may have been conducted for
these cases, independent and timely reviews as prescribed by Federal regulations should be
held to help ensure the welfare of the children. Documentation of the reviews should be

maintained.

administration of the Medical Assistance Program is the Florida Medicaid Management

Information System (FMMIS). The FMMIS is a claim processing system.

The entry of Medicaid Program provider, recipient, and claims data into the FMMIS is
performed by the FAHCA’s contracted fiscal agent (the Unisys Corporation). We noted that
the FAHCA requires annual audits (i.e., service auditor’s reports) of the FMMIS as
administered by the fiscal agent. Among other things, these audits address the fiscal agent’s
physical and data security operating procedures and related personnel practices. The most
recently issued service auditor’s report of the FMMIS was issued November 4, 1997, and
covered the period September 28, 1996, through September 26, 1997. That report disclosed
that several deficiencies noted in the previous service auditor’s report (which were disclosed in
Auditor General audit report No. 12983, paragraphs 470 through 474) had been resolved.
However, some of the deficiencies previously reported continued to exist. Specifically, the

service auditor’s report indicated the following:

e Data security policies and procedures had not been effectively established and formally
documented to assure the integrity of the system information. System access was
provided to employees based on a manager’s recommendation rather than from the data
custodian and designated security office. Additionally, data had not been classified in

terms of sensitivity.

e FMMIS access was not effectively controlled. Log-on identification numbers were
shared and certain identification numbers were in use by unidentified individuals.
Additionally, user passwords were not periodically changed and user access capabilities

were not periodically reviewed and compared to assigned job responsibilities.

suspended batches of claims did not always receive timely attention or completion of

processing.

The fiscal agent submitted a corrective action plan for the noted deficiencies to the FAHCA on
December 4, 1997. We recommend that the FAHCA continue their role in ensuring that

appropriate corrective actions are implemented.

Activities Allowed or Unallowed - DPAF Investigations. As disclosed in paragraph 4 and in
audit report No. 13060, the Auditor General’s Division of Public Assistance Fraud (DPAF),

working closely with the FAHCA and FDCFS, each year conducts a substantial number of
investigations of possible public assistance fraud relating to the Medicaid Program.
Investigations during the 1996-97 fiscal year resulted in the identification of 234 program
violations, with an estimated total value of $269,701, involving the apparent illegal receipt of
Medicaid Program benefits. These violations were referred to the appropriate State Attorneys’

Offices for consideration.

Allowable Costs/Cost Principles - Third Party Obligations. Title 42, Sections 433.135

through 433.154, Code of Federal Regulations, require states to have a system to identify
medical services that are the legal obligations of third parties, such as private health or casualty
insurers. Such third party resources are to be exhausted prior to the payment of claims with
Medicaid Program funds. Where third party liability is sought after the claim is paid,
reimbursement from the liable third party should be sought by the State. As a part of our
audit, we reviewed the FAHCA processes and procedures for identifying liable third parties,

ensuring that third party resources were exhausted prior to payment when applicable, and

ensuring that appropriate efforts were made to obtain reimbursement in those circumstances

whereby the claim had been paid.

One of the procedures implemented by the FAHCA was the submission of standard
questionnaires to recipients of Medicaid-funded medical services relating to trauma claims
(e.g., automobile accidents). In these questionnaires, the recipient is requested to indicate if
any claims had been filed with a private insurer by or on behalf of the recipient for the related
injuries (i.e., for which the applicable medical services had been paid from Medicaid Program
funds). Requested information included, for example, the name of the private insurer and the
related claim number. Any private insurance that covered the applicable trauma services
would be considered a liable third party and, therefore, reimbursable to the Medicaid Program.
We tested ten responses to these questionnaires sent by the FAHCA during the month of April
1997. We noted in nine of the responses that the recipient provided sufficient information,
relative to private insurance, that should have enabled the FAHCA to follow up and identify
any liable third parties. However, the FAHCA did not follow up on any of the provided
information for the purpose of identifying any liable third parties. In response to our audit
inquiry, the FAHCA provided a copy of a standard questionnaire to be used in following up on
responses to the initial questionnaire for the purpose of identifying liable third parties. The
FAHCA indicated that this procedure was implemented subsequent to January 1, 1998. The
lack of appropriate procedures during the audit period may have precluded the FAHCA from
receiving Medicaid Program reimbursements from liable third parties. We will review the
adequacy of the recently-implemented FAHCA procedures in our subsequent audits of the

program.

Special Test and Provisions - Provider Eligibility. Title 42, Sections 455.104 and 455.106,

Code of Federal Regulations, provide that the State Medicaid agency must require providers to
disclose specific information. Specifically, the provider must disclose, among other things, (1)
the name and address of each person with an ownership or control interest in the provider, (2)
the name of any other provider in which such individuals also have an ownership or control
interest, and (3) whether any such individuals have been convicted of a criminal offense related
to that person’s involvement in any Medicaid, Medicare, or Title XX Program since the
inception of those programs. These regulations also provide that the State Medicaid agency
shall not approve a provider agreement, and shall terminate an existing agreement, if the
provider fails to disclose the required ownership or control information. Furthermore, Section
409.907(8), Florida Statutes, requires a provider to submit proof that they are holding a valid
license, or operating certificate, as applicable, if required by the State or local jurisdiction in
which the provider is located or if required by the Federal Government. In regard to the State
of Florida Medicaid Program, the FAHCA fiscal agent (Unisys Corporation) performs part of
the provider enrollment function. The FAHCA is responsible for monitoring and overseeing

that function as performed by the fiscal agent.

As a part of our audit, we reviewed FAHCA’s records documenting that required ownership
information had been disclosed and proof of licensure provided by the 51 providers pertaining
to the 60 claims reviewed in connection with our other audit testing. Our test disclosed the
following 4 instances in which the FAHCA did not maintain adequate documentation

evidencing compliance with the above-noted provider disclosure and licensure requirements:

Special Tests and Provisions - Inpatient Hospital and Long-Term Care Facility Audits.

Pursuant to Title 42, Section 447.250, Code of Federal Regulations, hospitals and nursing
homes participating in the Medicaid Program are to be paid through rates that are reasonable
and adequate to meet the costs that must be incurred for efficient and economical services.
Accordingly, the State Medicaid agency must require those providers to file uniform cost
reports, which are to be used to establish payment rates for the respective providers. Periodic
audits of the financial and statistical data in the provider cost reports must be conducted. In
connection with our audit, we selected a sample of ten providers (eight hospital and two
nursing homes) and reviewed the related cost reports, audited and unaudited as applicable, and
payment rate calculations. For two of the ten providers, our review disclosed instances where
the payment rate calculations were not proper and/or not based on the most current cost data.

Specifically:

e For one hospital provider, the FAHCA used an incorrect figure in the rate calculation
process. Specifically, the figure used for “total fixed costs” was $1,000,000 more than
the amount in the cost report. This error caused the calculated inpatient reimbursement
per diem rate of $619.35 to be overstated by $28.02 (i.e., correct amount was $591.33).
Based on the hospital Medicaid inpatient days for the fiscal year covered by the cost
report, this error would have resulted in overpayments of approximately $52,000 for the
six-month period to be covered by that rate. Upon notification by our staff, this error

was corrected and the proper rate calculated.

e For another hospital provider, based on the unaudited cost report, an incorrect amount
was determined and used for “inpatient ancillary cost” in the rate calculation process.
Specifically, the amount of $58,810,808 was overstated by $596,805. As a result, the
inpatient reimbursement per diem rate was incorrect. Furthermore, we noted that the
rate calculation was based on the provider’s unaudited cost report, although an audit of

the provider had been conducted. Specifically, the reimbursement per diem rate had not

been adjusted for audited cost figures received by the FAHCA on September 30, 1996,

FAHCA provided records indicating that those audited cost figures had not been utilized
because FAHCA staff had not resolved incomplete information previously received (i.e.,
on December 17, 1993) pertaining to an audit of the provider’s fiscal year ended June
30, 1991. Subsequent to our inquiry, the FAHCA obtained the completed/corrected
information pertaining to that earlier audit and updated the provider’s reimbursement per
diem rate for all applicable periods. The period from the FAHCA’s receipt of that
earlier audit (i.e., December 17, 1993) to the receipt of the corrected/completed
information and revision of applicable reimbursement rates (i.e., April 9, 1998) was in
excess of four years. Notwithstanding that any over/underpayments should be recouped
from/paid to the applicable provider based on the revised rates, these circumstances
resulted in excess/insufficient payments that could have been avoided had the
corrected/complete information been timely obtained by the FAHCA. Furthermore, as
significant rate adjustments may adversely impact the cash position of a provider, it is

prudent that such adjustments be made timely so as to limit the period to which the

adjustments must be applied.

To ensure payments to hospital and nursing homes are based on properly determined rates, we
recommend that the FAHCA implement appropriate measures to ensure the accurate
calculation of those rates. Appropriate measures should also be taken to ensure that the most
accurate (e.g., audited) cost data available is timely obtained and used in those rate

calculations.

In audit report No. 12983, paragraph 476, we noted that the FDCFS (formerly the Florida
Department of Health and Rehabilitative Services) had overcharged the Medicaid Program
$25,422. This overcharge occurred because 100 percent of the 1995-96 fiscal year salary costs
of one FDCFS employee was charged to the Medicaid Program, although the employee
worked on other programs (i.e., the Food Stamp and Family Support Payments to States -
Assistance Payments Programs) in addition to the Medicaid Program. During our current
review, we noted that the FDCFS made appropriate correcting entries to reimburse the
Medicaid Program, on October 27, 1997, for those costs improperly charged for the 1995-96
fiscal year. Furthermore, effective January 10, 1997, the FDCFS changed the cost center to
which this employee’s salary costs were charged such that the applicable programs were
equitably charged subsequent to that date. Notwithstanding these corrective actions, we noted
that 100 percent of the employee’s salary costs incurred from July 1, 1996, through January 9,
1997, were inappropriately charged to the Medicaid Program. Based on the FDCFS estimates
of the employee’s time spent on Medicaid Program activities (i.e., 31 percent), the program
was overcharged $19,986 for that period. Subsequent to our inquiry, the FDCFS made
additional adjustments reimbursing the Medicaid Program for this overcharge. In its Summary
Schedule of Prior Audit Findings, dated November 19, 1997, the FDCFS indicated that it was
in the process of preparing guidelines for ensuring the equitable allocation of salary costs. We

recommend that the FDCFS continue efforts to complete and implement these guidelines.

services designed for pregnant women and women with dependent children. For the 1994 FFY
award, the State was required to expend not less than 5 percent of the award to increase such
services relative to the 1993 FFY award. For FFY grant awards subsequent to the 1994 FFY
award, the State is required to expend on such services no less than the amount expended by

Based on the regulation described above, the FDCFS was required to expend at least the same
amount for such services from the 1996 FFY award as was expended on those services from
the 1994 FFY award. This comparison showed that, as of September 30, 1997, the FDCFS
had charged $12,503,991 to the 1994 FFY award and $11,569,844 to the 1996 FFY award.
Assuming that the 1994 FFY benchmark of $12,503,991 is correct and the expenditures
charged to the 1996 FFY award are not revised (i.e., these amounts may depend on the
resolution of the noted reconciliation and “roll forward” issues), the FDCFS expended

$934,147 less than what was required.

As noted, a final determination as to whether this instance represents noncompliance cannot be
made until resolution of the reconciliation and “roll forward” issues. In response to our
inquiry, the FDCFS indicated that an independent CPA firm has been retained to perform a
thorough analysis of costs charged to the SAPT Program during the 1992-93 through 1995-96
State fiscal years for the purpose of rectifying discrepancies reported in prior years. The
FDCES indicated that this analysis, expected to be completed by June 30, 1998, will impact
previously reported data. Should this analysis confirm that amounts expended on women
services from the 1996 FFY award are less than amounts expended for those services from the
1994 FFY award, we recommend that the FDCFS take corrective actions to ensure that grant

funds are expended in accordance with governing level of effort requirements.

SAPT Program funds must be obligated within two years of the initial allotment by the

Opinion Qualification. Due to the unresolved issues pertaining to the reconciliation of the

subsequent grant awards, which precludes the FDCFS from determining and documenting
compliance with earmarking and period of availability requirements (see paragraphs 249, 252,

through 251); the FDCFS retaining unexpended 1995 FFY award funds for an excessive period
beyond the period of availability under the presumption that resolution of the noted
reconciliation and “roll forward” issues will not impact the 1995 FFY award expenditure
levels (see paragraphs 252 and 253); the FDCFS not expending administrative funds only for
allowable purposes and/or documenting those expenditures in accordance with governing
regulations (see paragraphs 246 and 247); and the FDCFS not documenting and/or accurately
reporting amounts in the annual report (see paragraphs 254 and 255), it is our opinion that the
FDCFS has not maintained a system of internal control to effectively ensure that the SAPT

Program has been administered materially in compliance with controlling laws and regulations.

Background

The Office of Management and Budget (OMB) Circular A-133 defines a cluster of programs as
a grouping of closely-related programs that share common compliance requirements. A cluster
of programs shall be considered as one program for determining major programs. The two
clusters of Federal programs for the State of Florida’s University System and Community
College System are those Federal programs categorized as student financial aid (SFA) and
research and development (R & D). Additional major programs of the State addressed in this
section include the Vocational Education - Basic Grants to States (CFDA No. 84.048) and Job
Training Partnership Act TPA) (CFDA No. 17.250).

Student financial aid includes those programs of general student assistance, such as those
authorized by Title IV of the Higher Education Act (HEA) of 1965, as amended, which are
administered by the United States Department of Education (USDOE), and similar programs
provided by other Federal agencies. It does not include programs which provide fellowships
or similar Federal awards to students on a competitive basis, or for specified studies or
research. The individual programs included in the SFA cluster are identified in a footnote to

the audited Schedule of Expenditures of Federal Awards. (See Section 2 of this report.)

Research and development means all research activities, both basic and applied, and all
development activities that are performed by a non-Federal entity. Research is defined as a
systematic study directed toward fuller scientific knowledge or understanding of the subject
studied. The term also includes activities involving the training of individuals in research
techniques where such activities utilize the same facilities as other research and development
activities and where such activities are not included in the instruction function. Development is
the systematic use of knowledge and understanding gained from research directed toward the
production of useful materials, devices, systems, or methods, including design and

development of prototypes and processes. The individual programs included in the R & D

OMB Circular A-133 requires that audit findings be reported by compliance requirements
within major program classification. Accordingly, the audit findings reported in the following
paragraphs are categorized according to major Federal awards program and within each major
program by type of compliance requirement. Applicable audit findings for each university and
community college are combined and reported under the appropriate compliance requirement
for a major program. These audit findings specify the individual universities and community

colleges to which the findings pertain.

STUDENT FINANCIAL AID CLUSTER

Eligibility

To be eligible for student financial aid, a student must meet various requirements as described
in Federal regulations. The determination of award amounts is based on financial need, which
is generally defined as the student’s cost of attendance minus financial resources reasonably
available. For Title IV, HEA programs, the financial resources available are generally
represented by the Expected Family Contribution (EFC). The EFC is computed by the.central
processor and included on the student’s Student Aid Report (SAR) and the Institutional Student
Information Report ([SIR) provided to the institution. In addition to eligibility requirements
and award limits, awards must be coordinated among the various programs and with other
Federal and non-Federal aid to assure that total aid is not awarded in excess of the student’s

financial need.

Our review of student eligibility, for the student financial aid programs (SFA) for the State of
Florida’s 10 State Universities and 28 Community Colleges, consisted of a sample of 1,940

students who received financial aid within the SFA cluster as defined by the revised Office of

Management and Budget (OMB) Circular A-133. Our audit tests disclosed audit findings
related to particular eligibility requirements, which are required to be reported pursuant to
OMB Circular A-133. These findings include reportable conditions as well as 36 instances of
noncompliance resulting in net questioned costs of $33,201 (total costs of $35,463 less $2,262
costs restored). Attachment 1 provides a summary of questioned costs for Eligibility. These

findings are discussed in detail under the following subheadings.

Overawards

Title 34, Sections 682 and 685, Code of Federal Regulations, specify loan limits for the
FFELP (CFDA No. 84.032) and FDSLP (CFDA No. 84.268) loans. Title 34, Section
682.603(d)(2)(ii), Code of Federal Regulations, states that a school may not certify a Stafford
(subsidized or unsubsidized) or PLUS loan application, or combination of loan applications,
for a loan amount that exceeds the student’s estimated cost of attendance less the student’s
estimated financial assistance for that period and, in the case of a Stafford loan that is eligible
for interest benefits, the borrower’s EFC for that period. Title 34, Section 685.301(a)(3),
Code of Federal Regulations, states that a school may not originate a Direct Subsidized, Direct
Unsubsidized, or Direct PLUS, or a combination of loans, for an amount that the school has
reason to know would result in the borrower exceeding the annual or maximum loan amounts
in Title 34, Section 685.203, Code of Federal Regulations. Pursuant to Title 34, Section
685.203(d)(1), Code of Federal Regulations, the aggregate unpaid principal amount of all
Direct Subsidized Loans and Federal Stafford Loans made to a student may not exceed
$23,000 in the case of any student who has not successfully completed a program of study at
the undergraduate level. Title 34, Section 685.200(a)(2)(i), Code of Federal Regulations,
requires that a Direct Subsidized Loan borrower must demonstrate financial need in order to be

eligible for the loan.

Guidelines included in The Federal Student Financial Aid Handbook, 1996-97, (Handbook)

provide that a student must demonstrate financial need to receive all SFA except for
unsubsidized loans under the FFELP and the FDSLP. Financial need is defined in the
Handbook as the difference between the student’s cost of attendance and his or her EFC. The
Handbook further provides that a school may certify an application for a subsidized FDSLP
only for the amount of need that remains after subtracting both the student’s EFC and
estimated financial assistance from his or her cost of attendance. Estimated financial assistance

is defined to include the value of a waiver of tuition and fees.

Title 34, Section 690.62(a), Code of Federal Regulations, requires that the amount of a
student’s Federal Pell Grant for an academic year should be based upon the payment and
disbursement schedules published by the Secretary for each award year. Pursuant to Title 34,
Section 668.16(b)(3), Code of Federal Regulations, a participant in any Title IV, HEA
program is required to communicate to the individual designated to be responsible for
administering Title IV, HEA programs all the information received by any institutional office
that bears on a student’s eligibility for Title IV, HEA program assistance. In addition, the
Handbook provides that total aid a student may receive from the SFA programs and other

sources when added to the student’s EFC may not exceed the student’s cost of attendance.

Our tests of overawards disclosed 13 instances in which loan amounts totaling $20,722 and 4
instances in which Federal Pell Grant amounts totaling $875 were awarded and paid but did not
demonstrate compliance with certain Federal regulations. These instances resulted in

questioned costs of $21,597 for the following institutions as described below.

Florida Agricultural and Mechanical University. As similarly reported in audit report No.

12983, Schedule 2, paragraph 14, the University awarded and disbursed to one undergraduate

student a subsidized FDSLP (CFDA No. 84.268) loan in the amount of $2,625, even though

University records showed that the student had previously borrowed $23,000 in Federal

Satisfactory Academic Progress

Title 34, Section 668.34, Code of Federal Regulations, provides that in order to be eligible to
receive SFA under Title IV, HEA, a student must be maintaining satisfactory progress in his
or her course of study according to the institution’s standards of satisfactory progress. Title
34, Section 668.16(e), Code of Federal Regulations, requires that an institution establish,
publish, and apply reasonable standards for measuring satisfactory progress. The Secretary
considers an institution’s standards to be reasonable if the standards include the following
elements: a qualitative component which consists of grades, work projects completed, or
comparable factors which are measured against a norm, and a quantitative component that
consists of a maximum time frame in which a student must complete his or her educational
program. Our tests disclosed 16 instances in which students did not maintain satisfactory

progress and continued to receive SFA. These instances resulted in net questioned costs of
